L6: ## Executive Summary
L7: The Applicant (CSL Behring) provided a Complete Response (CR) resubmission to biologics licensing application (BLA) 761367 for garadacimab, a fully human IgG4 monoclonal antibody that targets the catalytic subunit of Factor XIIa, for the proposed indication of prophylaxis to prevent attacks of hereditary angioedema (HAE) in adult and adolescent patients 12 years and older. The proposed recommended dosage of garadacimab is a 400mg subcutaneous (SC) injection loading dose followed by 200mg SC injection every month. HAE is a rare, autosomal dominant genetic condition, caused by reduced or absent functional C1 esterase inhibitor (C1-INH), which is clinically characterized by episodic and unpredictable attacks of angioedema involving the skin and/or submucosal surfaces, including the gastrointestinal tract, genitourinary tract, and/or throat and upper airway, which can be potentially life-threatening. Garadacimab, an FXIIa inhibitor, represents a novel therapeutic target for HAE.
L8: After an initial refusal to file due to unavailability of rabbit pyrogen test results, the original BLA application was resubmitted on October 13, 2023, and filed on December 12, 2023. The submitted clinical program was adequate to support the efficacy and safety of garadacimab for the proposed indication. The BLA included the appropriate preapproval nonclinical and clinical pharmacology studies. The pharmacology/toxicology, clinical
L9: pharmacology, statistical, and clinical review teams did not identify any issues that precluded approval of garadacimab in the initial BLA review cycle.1 However, the Office of Product Quality (OPQ) and the Office of Pharmaceutical Manufacturing Assessment (OPMA) review teams determined that the data submitted were not sufficient to conclude that the manufacture of garadacimab was well-controlled and would lead to a product that was pure and potent.2
L10: Therefore, the Agency issued a complete response letter (CRL) on October 7, 2024, citing microbiology, product quality, and facility inspection deficiencies.3 The microbiology deficiency was based on inadequate drug product (DP) manufacturing process design to minimize potential contamination of the sterile DP, specifically,
L11: Multiple product quality deficiencies were also identified including insufficient DP process control to ensure consistent product quality and insufficient DP process validation data to demonstrate consistent process performance. Lastly, a withhold approval was recommended for the DP manufacturing facility, CSL Behring GmbH, Marburg, Germany based on the pre-license inspection and facility compliance review.
L12: In this CR resubmission, the Applicant demonstrated that the manufacturing processes and overall control strategies for garadacimab were appropriately established to ensure consistency and quality of the final product and thus has adequately addressed the deficiencies outlined in the CRL. The Applicant and Agency have reached agreement on the product labeling that includes the carton &amp; container, Prescribing Information (PI), Patient Information, and Instructions for Use. The recommended regulatory action from all members of the multidisciplinary review team is Approval.
L13: ## Product Quality
L14: In this review cycle, a 704(a)(4) Records Request review was conducted for the DP manufacturing facility (CSL Behring GmbH, Marburg, Germany FEI 3003098680). OPMA determined that the facility inspection deficiencies outlined on Form FDA 483 had been adequately addressed.
L15: To address the microbiology deficiencies, the drug product (DP) manufacturing process
L16: To address the DP quality manufacturing process deficiencies, the Applicant implemented additional process controls and submitted supporting process validation data in the BLA resubmission. The Applicant also provided real time DP shipping validation data, specifically the Container Closure Integrity Testing (CCIT) and critical quality attributes (CQA) data of garadacimab after international transport in shipping containers. After reviewing the BLA resubmission, the OPQ determined that the Applicant adequately addressed the microbiology and product quality deficiencies outlined in the CRL. The

L21: ## Pharmacology/Toxicology
L22: No new nonclinical data were submitted or required in this CR resubmission. The updated proposed PI is acceptable. No pharmacology/toxicology issues preclude approval.
L23: ## Clinical Pharmacology
L24: No new clinical pharmacology data were provided in this CR resubmission. On resubmission, the Applicant addressed clinical pharmacology comments in the proposed PI. These included changes to Sections 7 (Drug Interactions), 12.2 (Pharmacodynamics), and 12.3 (Pharmacokinetics). The updated proposed PI is acceptable from a clinical pharmacology perspective. As concluded during the initial review cycle, no issues preclude approval from a clinical pharmacology perspective.

ORIGINAL
L26: ## Clinical/Statistics
L27: The initial BLA submission included results from a single, adequate and well-controlled phase 3 trial (Trial 3001). This trial was a 6-month, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial evaluating 64 adult and adolescent subjects with HAE Type I and II who received either garadacimab 200mg or placebo monthly.
L28: Trial 3001 met its primary endpoint, demonstrating a statistically significant reduction in the time-normalized number of investigator-confirmed HAE attacks per month. This endpoint is an established and clinically meaningful outcome for HAE prophylaxis development programs. The garadacimab group showed a mean of 0.27 attacks per month (95% CI: 0.05, 0.49) compared to 2.01 attacks per month (95% CI: 1.44, 2.57) in the placebo group. Substantial evidence of effectiveness of garadacimab in patients with HAE was established using data from Trial 3001 and confirmatory evidence based on mechanistic data. The available safety data demonstrated that garadacimab is safe for its intended use.
L29: In the BLA resubmission, no new efficacy data were required or submitted. The Applicant provided updated exposure and safety datasets through June 15, 2024 (previous data cutoff was May 22, 2023). The clinical review team analyzed the updated datasets and modified the drug exposure and frequency of injection site reactions in Section 6 of the PI, accordingly. Section 14 of the updated proposed PI is acceptable from the statistical and
L30: 4 OPQ Executive Summary dated May 20, 2025:
https://darrts.fda.gov/darrts/faces/ViewDocument?documentId=090140af807ba780
L31: clinical perspective. No clinical or statistical issues preclude approval. The benefit-risk assessment of garadacimab for the prophylaxis of HAE attacks in adults and pediatric patients 12 years of age and older remains favorable.

L72: ## I. Executive Summary
L73: ## 1. Summary of Regulatory Action
L74: The Applicant, CSL Behring, submitted a BLA for garadacimab (proposed tradename: ANDEMBRY), a fully human IgG4 monoclonal antibody that targets the catalytic subunit of Factor XIIa, for prophylaxis to prevent attacks of hereditary angioedema (HAE) in adult and adolescent patients 12 years and older at a dose of 200 mg subcutaneous (SC) monthly following a 400 mg SC loading dose. The BLA was reviewed by a multidisciplinary review team. The clinical, statistical, clinical pharmacology, and pharmacology/toxicology review teams did not identify any issues that preclude approval. However, the Office of Product Quality and Office of Pharmaceutical Manufacturing Assessment review teams have determined that the data submitted in this application are not sufficient to conclude that the manufacture of garadacimab is well-controlled and will lead to a product that is pure and potent, and therefore, recommend a complete response.
L75: The substantial evidence of effectiveness of garadacimab in patients with HAE was established using data from a single adequate and well-controlled trial (CSL312_3001, hereafter referred to as trial 3001) and confirmatory evidence (CE) based on mechanistic data. Trial 3001 demonstrated a statistically significant reduction in the frequency of acute HAE attacks with garadacimab treatment compared to placebo, as measured by the primary endpoint of the time-normalized number of HAE attacks/month over a 6-month Treatment Period. This was supported by the results of secondary endpoints, such as the number of attack-free subjects, moderate-to-severe HAE attacks, and HAE attacks requiring on-demand therapy. The CE providing strong mechanistic support includes the well-established pathophysiology of the disease and the mechanism of action of the therapy. In vitro and in vivo pharmacology data show that garadacimab only antagonizes FXIIa enzymatic activities, prolongs aPTT clotting time in standard human plasma, and inhibits FXIIa-mediated kallikrein activity in a dose-dependent fashion. Extrapolation of efficacy from adults with HAE to adolescent patients 12 to 17 years of age with HAE is supported by the identical disease pathophysiology, mechanism of action, anticipated treatment response, and clinical manifestations across age groups.
L76: The available safety data demonstrated that garadacimab is safe for its intended use. Common adverse reactions include nasopharyngitis and abdominal pain. The safety profile for garadacimab is acceptable for an HAE population and can be adequately managed through labeling and further evaluated during routine pharmacovigilance.
L77: The BLA includes appropriate preapproval nonclinical studies, and there is no anticipated need for additional nonclinical studies or clinical pharmacology studies as postmarketing requirements. The submitted clinical program is adequate to support the efficacy and safety of garadacimab for the prophylaxis to prevent attacks of HAE in patients 12 years of age and older. The clinical review team concludes that the reduction in acute HAE attacks observed with garadacimab treatment compared to placebo outweighs the risks and that garadacimab will provide a novel treatment option in the armamentarium for HAE prophylaxis.
L78: The action for this application will be a Complete Response.
L79: ## 2. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L80: ## 2.1. Benefit-Risk Framework
L81: Table 2. Benefit-Risk Framework
L82: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of condition	Type I and II hereditary angioedema (HAE) is a rare condition caused by autosomal dominant mutations in the SERPING1 gene, resulting in deficiency or dysfunction of C1-esterase inhibitor (C1-INH) protein.
Approximately 85% of patients have Type I HAE, characterized by low production of normal C1-INH protein. The remaining 15% of patients have Type II HAE, characterized by production of dysfunctional C1-INH. Types I and II have the same clinical phenotypes and responses to available treatments.
Insufficient quantities of functional C1-INH lead to dysregulation of the kallikrein-kinin system, a plasma protease cascade initiated by Factor XII and culminating in the generation of bradykinin. Uncontrolled bradykinin production leads to increased vascular permeability and the cutaneous/submucosal swelling seen in acute HAE attacks.
Acute HAE attacks are sporadic, recurrent, unpredictable and typically self-resolve after 48-72 h. Attacks often involve the GI/GU tract, extremities (hands/feet), and face/upper airway. Attacks are associated with significant morbidity due to pain, impact on daily functioning, and disfigurement and can be life-threatening in cases of laryngeal edema resulting in airway compromise.
HAE is a rare, genetic, potentially life-threatening disease characterized by unpredictable, recurrent, self-limited skin and submucosal swelling attacks. The pathophysiology of the disease is well characterized.
L83: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Current treatment options	No cure exists; however, there are several approved therapies for prophylaxis as well as for on-demand treatment of acute attacks.
Therapies for prophylaxis include plasma-derived C1-INH replacement therapies, oral attenuated androgens, and plasma kallikrein inhibitors. Available treatment options include oral and injectable (IV and SC) products.
Many patients continue to have breakthrough acute HAE attacks despite treatment prophylaxis.
Although there are numerous approved prophylactic therapies for patients with HAE, the availability of additional treatment options from a new pharmacologic class and with an infrequent dosing regimen is desirable.
Benefit	Efficacy is based on the results from a single, adequate and well-controlled trial (3001) in 64 adults and adolescents with Type I or II HAE. Treatment with garadacimab resulted in statistically significant and clinically persuasive reductions in the primary endpoint, number of HAE attacks per month, throughout the 6-month Treatment Period compared to placebo.
Clinically meaningful secondary endpoints, such as number of attack-free subjects, number of moderate to severe HAE attacks, and number of attacks requiring acute treatment, supported the beneficial treatment effect of garadacimab compared to placebo.
Efficacy in adolescents is based on extrapolation from adults.
Garadacimab demonstrated statistically significant and clinically relevant treatment benefits on the reduction of number of acute HAE attacks in adult and adolescent subjects with Types I and II HAE.
Risk and risk management	TEAEs that were serious, severe, or led to treatment discontinuation were rare.
Common TEAEs were nasopharyngitis and abdominal pain. The majority of TEAEs were mild or moderate in severity.
The safety profile of garadacimab for the proposed HAE population and intended use is favorable. The potential risks can be managed through labeling and routine pharmacovigilance.
No REMS is proposed.
L84: Abbreviations: GI, gastrointestinal; GU, genitourinary; IV, intravenous; REMS, risk evaluation and mitigation strategy; SC, subcutaneous; TEAE, treatment-emergent adverse event
L85: ## 2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
L86: HAE is a rare, autosomal dominant condition, most often caused by pathogenic variants in the *SERPING-1* gene that encodes C1-esterase inhibitor (C1-INH), a serine protease-inhibitor involved in the kallikrein-kinin pathway as well as the classical and lectin complement, contact system/intrinsic coagulation, and fibrinolytic pathways. Under homeostatic circumstances, activated Factor XII (FXIIa) triggers the kallikrein-kinin system, resulting in the downstream production of bradykinin, a potent vasodilator and enhancer of vascular permeability. C1-INH normally serves as an inhibitory check on the system by inhibiting both kallikrein and FXIIa; in the absence of sufficient quantities of functional C1-INH, bradykinin production is relatively unchecked and leads to recurrent swelling (or angioedema) of the skin and submucosa. Clinically, HAE is characterized by episodic, spontaneous, unpredictable attacks of nonhistaminergic angioedema involving the skin and submucosal surfaces of the face, gastrointestinal tract, genitourinary tract, extremities (hands/feet), and/or throat and upper airway, the last of which can be particularly serious and potentially life-threatening. Several products are approved for the prophylaxis of HAE attacks as well as for the treatment of acute HAE attacks; however, FXIIa represents a novel therapeutic target.
L87: To support the BLA for garadacimab, the Applicant submitted the results of a single, adequate and well-controlled phase 3 trial, 3001. Trial 3001 was a 6-month, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial evaluating 64 adult and adolescent subjects with HAE Type I or II who received garadacimab 200 mg or placebo SC every month. The primary efficacy endpoint was the time-normalized number of investigator-confirmed HAE attacks per month, an established, clinically meaningful outcome for HAE prophylaxis development programs. Trial 3001 met its primary endpoint, demonstrating a statistically significant and clinically meaningful reduction in the number of HAE attacks per month in the garadacimab group (mean=0.27; 95% CI=0.05, 0.49) compared to the placebo group (mean=2.01; 95% CI=1.44, 2.57). Secondary endpoints—such as the proportion of subjects with a \ge 50\% reduction in attacks and the numbers of attack-free subjects, moderate-to-severe HAE attacks, and HAE attacks requiring acute on-demand treatment—demonstrated results that were supportive of the primary endpoint, all favoring garadacimab treatment.
L88: Given the rarity of HAE, a single adequate and well-controlled trial with support from CE is considered sufficient for demonstration of substantial evidence of effectiveness for the proposed indication. The CE consists of strong mechanistic data from the well-understood pathophysiology of this genetic disease and the mechanism of action of the drug. As described above, the kallikrein-kinin pathway and its components, and the mechanism by which pathologic variants in the *SERPING-1* gene lead to bradykinin overproduction and the clinical manifestations of HAE have been well-characterized. Garadacimab inhibits FXIIa, a key protease in the activation of the contact/kallikrein-kinin system pathway, and thereby reduces the downstream production of bradykinin. As bradykinin is the main mediator of angioedema in HAE, and there are no known alternative pathways for bradykinin production in C1-INH-deficient patients (i.e., with Types I and II HAE), inhibition of FXIIa and its activation of the kallikrein-kinin pathway is expected to result in fewer angioedema episodes.
L89: The safety assessment of garadacimab revealed no concerning safety signals that would preclude approval. No deaths occurred in the clinical development program. Serious adverse events,
L90: severe adverse events, and adverse events leading to treatment discontinuation were rare with no major imbalances between treatment groups. Overall, treatment-emergent adverse events were predominantly mild or moderate in severity, with the most common adverse reactions being nasopharyngitis and abdominal pain. As with other HAE products that target the contact system/kallikrein-kinin pathway, prolongation of the coagulation parameters activated partial thromboplastin time and prothrombin time were asymptomatic and not associated with an increased risk of bleeding or thromboembolism. Potential risks can be addressed through labeling and routine pharmacovigilance.
L91: Although trial 3001 enrolled six adolescent subjects (four garadacimab, two placebo), the efficacy and safety of garadacimab in adolescent patients 12 to &lt;18 years of age is primarily based on extrapolation from adults and matching pharmacokinetic exposure. Extrapolation is supported by the same underlying pathophysiology and clinical manifestations of disease, mechanism of action of garadacimab, and similar expected response to treatment across age groups.
L92: Overall, the review team concludes that the benefit-risk assessment is favorable and supports the approval of garadacimab 200 mg SC monthly for the proposed indication of prophylaxis to prevent HAE attacks in adults and adolescents 12 years of age and above.
L93: ## II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
L94: ## 3. Introduction
L95: This review serves as the interdisciplinary assessment for BLA 761367. Garadacimab is a new molecular entity, first-in-class, fully human IgG4 monoclonal antibody targeting the catalytic subunit of activated Factor XII (FXIIa). The proposed indication is for prophylaxis to prevent attacks of hereditary angioedema (HAE) in adult and adolescent patients aged 12 years and older. The proposed dosage is an initial loading dose of 400 mg administered subcutaneously (SC) on the first day of treatment followed by a maintenance dose of 200 mg SC every month.
L96: HAE is a rare genetic condition with an estimated prevalence of 1 in 10,000 to 50,000 people (~6000 to 30,000 in the United States) (USHAA 2024). The overwhelming majority of patients with HAE have pathogenic variants in the *SERPING-1* gene that encodes C1-esterase inhibitor (C1-INH), resulting in either low levels of C1-INH (Type I) or normal levels of dysfunctional C1-INH (Type II) (Busse et al. 2021). A very small fraction of patients has Type III HAE caused by other genetic mutations (e.g., Factor XII (FXII), angiopoietin-1, plasminogen (PLG)) and exhibit normal levels and function of C1-INH. The inheritance of HAE is autosomal dominant; however, approximately 25% of patients have de novo mutations. Patients with HAE experience recurrent episodes of swelling in the hands/feet, genitals, gastrointestinal tract, face and/or throat that typically lasts for 2 to 3 days, but occasionally up to 5 days. Symptom onset is typically in childhood, with 50% of patients experiencing angioedema episodes by age 10 years, and symptoms usually increase after puberty. There is a high degree of inter- and intrapatient variability in both the frequency and severity of attacks. Characteristically, HAE symptoms do not respond to mast-cell targeted treatments such as antihistamines, corticosteroids, or epinephrine. Pharmacologic treatment of HAE consists of access to on-demand therapies to treat acute episodes of angioedema and consideration for long-term prophylaxis on an individual basis. Approved therapies for prophylaxis and on-demand treatment of acute attacks generally consist of products that target the kallikrein-kinin pathway (i.e., C1-INH replacements, plasma kallikrein inhibitors, bradykinin B2-receptor antagonists). Danazol, an attenuated androgen, is also approved for prophylaxis, although its mechanism of action in HAE is unclear. Garadacimab represents a novel therapeutic for HAE.
L97: The Applicant submitted an IND application (IND 139936) in 2018 to investigate garadacimab for the prophylaxis of HAE attacks. Garadacimab received Fast Track Designation on December 14, 2021, and orphan drug designation on April 30, 2020. The Agency refused to file the initial BLA submission on August 28, 2023, due to a lack of the required Rabbit Pyrogen Test (21 CFR 610.13). The Applicant resubmitted the BLA on October 13, 2023, after addressing the deficiencies outlined in the Refuse-to-File letter. The application was subsequently filed and is the subject of this review. Refer to Section 13 for the complete regulatory history.
L98: During the course of the application review, the review team identified two key efficacy review issues and three key safety review issues, as listed below. In-depth analyses and assessments of the efficacy and safety review issues can be found in Sections 6.3 and 7.7, respectively.

L107: ## 3.2. Approach to the Clinical Review

Trial 3001 served as the primary basis for efficacy and safety of garadacimab. Trial 2001 provided data to support dose selection as well as supportive efficacy and safety results. Additionally, the ongoing open-label extension (OLE) study 3002 provided data on the long-term safety of garadacimab.

L118: ## 5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics, and Clinical Pharmacology
L119: ## 5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential Effectiveness
L120: Garadacimab was shown to only antagonize FXIIa enzymatic activities, displaying 100% inhibition of FXIIa catalytic activity for both the \alpha and \beta fragments, meaning that zymogen FXII functions are negligibly impacted. In an in vitro chromogenic assay, garadacimab inhibited the enzymatic activity of human plasma-derived FXIIa and \betaFXIIa at equivalent levels (half maximal inhibitory concentration~15nM). Garadacimab prolonged the activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) clotting time in standard human plasma.
L121: Garadacimab did not significantly inhibit other serum proteases tested when incubated at an enzyme: antibody ratio of up to 1:500 and exhibited half maximal inhibitory concentration values &gt;1\mu\text{M} for all enzymes tested.
L122: In an in vivo angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitor (captopril)-induced edema murine model, garadacimab significantly inhibited the angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitor-induced increase of vascular permeability. Complete ablation of vascular leakage was observed at doses of \ge 0.5 \text{ mg/kg}.
L123: The established pharmacologic class for garadacimab has been designated as “FXIIa inhibitor” (monoclonal antibody) based on the in vitro and in vivo pharmacology data.
L124: ## 5.2. Clinical Pharmacology / Pharmacokinetics
L125: Key clinical pharmacology information about garadacimab is summarized in Table 5.
L126: **Table 5. Summary of Clinical Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetics**
L127: Characteristic	Drug Information
Established pharmacologic class (EPC)	Pharmacologic Activity
Activated Factor XII (FXIIa) inhibitor (monoclonal antibody).
Mechanism of action	Garadacimab is a fully human IgG4/lambda recombinant monoclonal antibody (molecular weight ~148 kDa) that binds to the catalytic domain of activated Factor XII (FXIIa) and inhibits its catalytic activity. Inhibition of FXIIa prevents the activation of pre-kallikrein to kallikrein and the generation of bradykinin, which is associated with inflammation and swelling in HAE attacks, thus blocking the cascade of events leading to an HAE attack.
Active moieties	Garadacimab
QT prolongation	Clinical and nonclinical ECG findings are consistent with the fact that antibodies have a low likelihood of direct ion channel interaction and therefore are not expected to cause concentration-dependent prolongation of the QTc interval. However, ECG data collected by the Applicant cannot serve as a substitute for a thorough QT study. As garadacimab is a monoclonal antibody, a thorough QT/QTc study is not warranted. For additional details, refer to the QT-IRT review in DARRTS dated April 25, 2024.
Bioanalysis	General Information
Plasma concentrations of garadacimab were measured using an enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay (ELISA). Bioanalytical methods were adequately validated.
Healthy subjects versus patients	Garadacimab PK in patients with HAE is consistent with that in healthy subjects
Drug exposure at steady state following the therapeutic dosing regimen (or single dose, if more relevant for the drug)	Table 6. Mean (SD) Garadacimab Systemic Exposure Following a Single 200 mg SC Dose in Healthy Subjects

AUC0-inf (\mu\text{g}\cdot\text{h/mL})	11470 (4103)

Abbreviations: AUC0-inf, area under the concentration-time curve from time 0 to infinity; Cmax, maximum plasma concentration; SC, subcutaneous; SD, standard deviation
L128: Characteristic	Drug Information
Range of effective dose(s) or exposure	
In HAE subjects, the mean (SD) steady-state trough concentrations of garadacimab ranged from 8.09 (4.28) µg/mL to 8.69 (3.93) µg/mL following 400 mg loading dose with 200 mg once monthly maintenance dosing regimen.
In dose-ranging trial 2001, three dosing levels of garadacimab of 75, 200, or 600 mg SC Q4W were evaluated in the 13-week placebo-controlled Treatment Period. A reduction in the mean time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month was observed for all dosing levels relative to placebo without a clear dose-response trend observed between 200 mg and 600 mg.
In the pivotal phase 3 trial 3001, subjects randomized to garadacimab 200 mg SC Q1M with 400 mg loading dose had significantly lower mean time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month relative to subjects randomized to placebo.
Exposure-response analyses for efficacy based on the relative risk of an HAE attack indicated that dosages ≥200 mg SC once monthly are predicted to yield steady-state plasma trough concentrations associated with a 90% reduction in HAE attack rate compared to placebo. Increasing the dose beyond 200 mg is not predicted to result in significant further reductions in HAE attack rate.

Maximally tolerated dose or exposure	Trial 2001 evaluated SC dosing regimens up to 600 mg Q4W in patients with HAE. In healthy subjects, single IV doses up to 10 mg/kg (median dose of 879 mg) were studied.
Dose proportionality	Garadacimab exposure increased in a dose-proportional manner between IV doses of 0.1 and 10 mg/kg and SC doses of 1 and 10 mg/kg.
Accumulation	No additional accumulation was observed at steady state following the proposed 400 mg loading dose with 200 mg monthly maintenance dosing regimen.
Time to achieve steady-state	Steady state is achieved by the third dose (i.e., after SC administration of the initial loading dose of 400 mg and the first 200 mg dose).
Bridge between to-be-marketed and clinical trial/study formulations	
In early clinical trials (i.e., 1001, 1003, and 2001), a 100 mg/mL formulation in a vial presentation was used. The remaining studies used the to-be-marketed 170 mg/mL formulation in a PFS presentation. A comparison of steady state trough concentrations across trials 2001 (100 mg/mL) and 3001 and study 3002 (170 mg/mL) indicated that formulation has no impact on garadacimab PK.
The final, to-be-marketed formulation in the PFS-NSD presentation was used in the pivotal clinical trial (3001). The proposed AI presentation was not used in trial 3001. The PFS-NSD and AI presentations were bridged in relative bioavailability study 1004 and bioequivalence was demonstrated.

Estimated absolute bioavailability (95% CI) is 39 (34, 43)%
Tmax	Median Tmax ranged from 5.5 to 7 days

Following administration of a single 200 mg SC dose in healthy subjects, the mean (SD) apparent volume of distribution is 11.8 (5.17) L

Clearance	Following administration of a single 200 mg SC dose in healthy subjects, the mean (SD) apparent clearance is 0.020 (0.008) L/h
Half-life	Mean terminal elimination half-life is about 17 to 20 days
Metabolic pathway(s)	Not applicable
Primary excretion pathways (% dose)	Not applicable
Body weight	Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight is a significant covariate affecting the PK of garadacimab such that garadacimab exposure is increased in patients with lower body weight. Body weight-based differences in exposures are not clinically relevant and no dose adjustments based on body weight are recommended.
Age	Age (12 to 73 years) did not meaningfully influence the PK of garadacimab
Renal impairment	No dedicated studies have been conducted to evaluate the impact of renal impairment on the PK of garadacimab. Per population PK analysis, renal impairment (eGFR \ge90 mL/min/1.73 m2 [normal, N=145]; 60 to <90 mL/min/1.73 m2 [mild, N=26]; and 30 to <60 mL/min/1.73 m2 [moderate, N=2]) did not impact the PK of garadacimab.
Hepatic impairment	No dedicated studies have been conducted to evaluate the impact of hepatic impairment on the PK of garadacimab. Per population PK analysis, bilirubin (4 to 13\mumol/L – 10th and 90th percentiles, respectively, in the observed dataset) did not significantly impact garadacimab clearance.
Inhibition/induction of metabolism	Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Perpetrator)
Not applicable
Inhibition/induction of transporter systems	Not applicable
Bioanalysis	Immunogenicity
The immunogenicity assay was a bridging assay that used an acid dissociation treatment step. Acid-treated samples were incubated in the presence of biotinylated and sulfo-tagged garadacimab, which form bridging complexes with ADAs. A tiered approach was used in which samples were screened for ADAs, confirmed positive, and then evaluated for titers.
Incidence	In pivotal trial 3001, the ADA incidence was 2.6% (1/39) in patients who received garadacimab during the 6-month Treatment Period. Among all patients with HAE who received the proposed garadacimab dosing regimen in trial 3001 and study 3002, the incidence of treatment-emergent ADAs was 3.9% (5/129).
Clinical impact	There was no apparent impact of immunogenicity on the PK, efficacy, or safety of garadacimab. Overall, few patients with HAE tested positive for ADAs, precluding a definitive assessment of the impact of ADAs.

L132: ## 6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
L133: ## 6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential Effectiveness
L134: The proposed dose of garadacimab is 200 mg SC every month, with an initial 400 mg loading dose. The dose and dosing regimen were investigated in a phase 2 dose-ranging trial (2001) and a phase 3 pivotal trial (3001), and by pharmacokinetic (PK) modeling.
L135: ## Trial 2001
L136: Trial 2001, described in detail in Section 16.2, evaluated four dosing regimens: 75 mg SC, 200 mg SC, and 600 mg SC every 4 weeks (Q4W) with different intravenous (IV) loading doses (40, 100, and 300 mg, respectively), and 400 mg SC every 2 weeks (open label). The primary endpoint of time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month was compared to placebo for the 200 mg and 600 mg treatment arms (see Table 117). The primary endpoint was also compared exploratorily between the 200 mg and 600 mg arms. Both the 200 mg and 600 mg arms met the primary objective and showed significant reductions in the time-normalized number of HAE attacks, but injection site reactions (ISRs) were more common in the 600 mg arm compared to the 200 mg arm. In March 2020, a protocol amendment was submitted by the Applicant, and all subjects with C1-INH (Type I or Type II) HAE were switched to the 200 mg arm.
L137: Clinical and PK data from trial 2001 were used to support dose selection for trial 3001. The garadacimab concentration-effect relationships were explored based on the relative risk of an HAE attack using maximal-efficacy model. The model-predicted relative risk of an HAE attack as a function of steady-state garadacimab plasma trough concentration is shown in Figure 1. Model predictions evaluated dosing regimens in which garadacimab was administered SC every month and included administration of an initial loading dose of twofold the monthly dose. Results indicated that dosages \ge200 mg administered SC once monthly yielded a steady-state median plasma trough concentration associated with a ~90% reduction, on average, in HAE attack rate compared to placebo. Model predictions also indicated that a plateau phase for reduction of HAE attack rate was reached following the 200-mg dose.
L138: Figure 1. Relative Risk of an HAE Attack as a Function of Garadacimab Concentration
L139: Source: Summary of Clinical Pharmacology, Figure 14.
L140: In the top panel, the blue line is the model prediction, and the gray ribbon is the approximate 95% CI. In the bottom panel, the box plots indicate distribution of steady state garadacimab C_{\text{trough}} by dose. The box represents the IQR, the line in the box represents the median, and the whiskers represent 1.5 \times \text{IQR}. Red dashed lines in both panels correspond to relative risk of HAE attacks of 0.50, 0.25, and 0.10 (left to right). Each dosing regimen includes an initial SC loading dose of twofold the Q1M dose.
L141: Abbreviations: CI, confidence interval; CSL312, garadacimab; C_{\text{trough}}, plasma trough concentration; HAE, hereditary angioedema; IQR, interquartile range; Q1M, once monthly; SC, subcutaneously
L142: Based on the results of the phase 2 dose-ranging trial (2001) and assessment of exposure-response (E-R) relationships for efficacy, selection of the 200-mg dose for the phase 3 trial (3001) appears to be reasonable.
L143: ## **Trial 3001**
L144: The 200 mg monthly maintenance dosing regimen (with a 400 mg loading dose) was further evaluated in the 6-month, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, phase 3 trial (3001). The 200-mg dose met the primary endpoint of time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month and all key secondary efficacy endpoints, demonstrating an improvement over placebo (see Sections 6.2.4.3 and 6.2.4.4).
L145: Additionally, the phase 3 PK data were incorporated into the E-R analyses for efficacy based on the relative risk of HAE attacks. The model-predicted relative risk of an HAE attack as a function of simulated steady-state garadacimab exposure (area under the concentration-time curve (AUC) over one dosing interval at steady state) is shown in **Figure 2**. Simulated steady-state exposure was derived from the proposed dosing regimen consisting of a 400 mg SC loading dose followed by 200 mg SC once monthly. Results indicate that at steady state, the area under the plasma concentration-time curve over one dosing interval at steady state (AUC_{\tau,ss}) following the 200 mg once monthly maintenance dose yielded a reduction in the relative risk of HAE attacks near the plateau of the curve. Also, population predictions of exposure indicated that about 75% of subjects will achieve an at least 90% reduction in the HAE attack rate compared to baseline.
L146: **Figure 2. Relative Risk of an HAE Attack as a Function of Garadacimab Steady-State AUC_{\tau,ss}**
L147: Source: Summary of Clinical Pharmacology, Figure 21.
L148: Notes: In the top panel, the simulated mean HAE relative risk is shown as the blue line with an approximate 95% CI for the mean in gray. The bottom panel shows the density of simulated AUC_{\tau,ss} where the boxplot indicates the 25th, 50th, and 75th percentiles with whiskers extending to 1.5 \times IQR.
L149: Abbreviations: AUC_{\tau,ss}, area under the plasma concentration-time curve over one dosing interval at steady state; CI, confidence interval; HAE, hereditary angioedema; IQR, interquartile range
L150: ## **Loading Dose Justification**
L151: The proposed dosing regimen includes an initial loading dose of 400 mg SC. This loading dose was incorporated into the regimen administered to subjects randomized to garadacimab in the phase 3 trial (3001). E-R analyses for efficacy (reduction in HAE attack rate) shown above were
L152: used to identify exposure thresholds to reach a 90% relative risk reduction based on area under the concentration-time curve during the dosing interval, maximum plasma concentration (C_{max}), and minimum plasma concentration. Inclusion of the loading dose was justified by demonstrating that the probability of exceeding the 90% responder threshold after the first dose was greater if a loading dose was administered relative to a regimen in which no loading dose was given (see Table 7).
L153: **Table 7. Loading Dose Justification: Probability of Achieving Exposure Exceeding a 90% Reduction in HAE Attack Rate After the First Dose**
L154: Regimen	Probability (Exposure \ge 90% Responder Threshold)
AUC_{\tau}	C_{max}	C_{min}
With loading dose	0.853	0.854	0.889
Without loading dose	0.397	0.472	0.414
L155: Source: Summary of Clinical Pharmacology, Table 11.
L156: Abbreviations: AUC_{\tau}, area under the plasma concentration-time curve over one dosing interval; C_{max}, maximum plasma concentration; C_{min}, minimum plasma concentration; HAE, hereditary angioedema
L157: Overall, the data from trials 2001 and 3001 supported the proposed dosing regimen of a 400 mg loading dose administered SC followed by a 200-mg dose administered SC once monthly.
L158: ## 6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended To Demonstrate Efficacy
L159: ## 6.2.1. Design and Conduct, Trial 3001
L160: Results of the pivotal trial (3001) served as the basis for efficacy evaluation. This was a 6-month, randomized (3:2 to garadacimab: placebo), double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel-group, multicenter (9 U.S. sites and 19 ex-U.S. sites), phase 3 trial evaluating 200 mg garadacimab administered SC once monthly in adults (aged 18 to 69 years) and adolescents (aged 12 to 17 years) with C1-INH (Type I or Type II) HAE who had \ge2 HAE attacks during the Run-in Period. This trial had a Screening Period of up to 1 month, a Run-in Period of up to 2 months, a 6-month Treatment Period, and a 2-month Follow-up Period (Figure 3).
L161: **Figure 3. Trial Design, Trial 3001**
L162: Source: Clinical Study Report (dated October 6, 2022), Figure 9-1, p. 21.
L163: Abbreviations: CSL312, garadacimab; HAE, hereditary angioedema; OLE, open-label extension; q1m, once monthly; SC, subcutaneous
L164: After obtaining informed consent (or assent for minors), subjects entered the Screening Period to determine eligibility for study enrollment. Individuals with FXII/PLG (Type III) HAE were not enrolled. Screened subjects who met all the inclusion criteria, and none of the exclusion criteria, entered the Run-in Period (see Section 6.2.2 for the key inclusion and exclusion criteria). If a subject did not meet all the criteria for entering the Run-in Period within 30 days, the subject could be rescreened once.
L165: Following screening, subjects entered the Run-in Period, which lasted at least 1 month and up to 2 months, to confirm their underlying disease status and assess their eligibility for participation in the Treatment Period. Subjects had to experience \ge 2 HAE attacks during the Run-in Period to enter the Treatment Period. Subjects who experienced \ge 2 HAE attacks during the required first month could exit the Run-in Period and enter the Treatment Period. Subjects were not allowed to take prophylaxis during the Run-in Period. Adults on HAE prophylaxis had a minimum 2-week (for C1-INH products, androgens, and antifibrinolytics) or 3-month (for lanadelumab) Washout Period before entering the Run-in Period (subjects not on prophylaxis did not require a Washout Period). Adolescents on routine (long-term) HAE prophylaxis were excluded from the trial.
L166: In the Treatment Period, 64 eligible participants were randomized 3:2 to receive 200 mg garadacimab or matching placebo once monthly for 6 months. Randomization accounted for age (\le 17 years; &gt; 17 years) and, for adults, baseline attack rate, defined as the time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month during the Run-in Period (1 to &lt; 3; \ge 3).
L167: For details regarding the collection and reporting of HAE data, and the investigator confirmation of HAE attacks, see Section 15.1.1.
L168: The overall design of the trial is consistent with previous programs for HAE prophylaxis. The duration of the Treatment Period (6 months) is acceptable for this program.
L169: Participants who completed the Treatment Period were eligible to enroll in a phase 3b, OLE study (3002; see Sections 7.6.2 and 16.2).
L170: ## **Primary Objective and Endpoint**
L171: The primary objective of this trial was to evaluate the efficacy of garadacimab as prophylaxis to prevent HAE attacks in subjects with C1-INH (Type I or Type II) HAE. The primary efficacy endpoint was the time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month during the Treatment Period compared to matched placebo (only investigator-confirmed HAE attacks, as defined in Section 15.1.1, were included). The primary endpoint was calculated per subject as:
L172: [\text{number of HAE attacks} \div \text{length of subject treatment in days}] \times 30.4375
L173: Length of subject treatment (in days) was calculated as:
L174: [\text{date of Study Visit Day 182 or the End-of-Study date (whichever occurred first)} \\ - \text{date of Study Visit Day 1 of Treatment Period} + 1]
L175: ## **Secondary and Exploratory Objectives and Endpoints**
L176: The secondary trial objectives included characterizing the clinical efficacy of garadacimab in the prophylactic treatment of HAE. Only investigator-confirmed HAE attacks, as defined in
L177: Section 15.1.1, were included in the efficacy evaluation. The key secondary efficacy endpoints were:
L178: - Percentage reduction in the mean time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month during the Treatment Period.
- Number of subjects who did not experience an HAE attack (i.e., were attack-free) in the first 3 months of the Treatment Period.
- Percentage of subjects with *good* or *excellent* responses to the Subject's Global Assessment of Response to Therapy (SGART) at the end of the Treatment Period.
L179: The first key secondary endpoint above was calculated as:
L180: 100 \times \left[ \frac{\text{mean time-normalized number of HAE attacks for garadacimab} - \text{mean time-normalized number of HAE attacks for placebo}}{\text{mean time-normalized number of HAE attacks for placebo}} \right]
L181: The other secondary efficacy endpoints were:
L182: - Percentage reduction (\ge 50\%, \ge 70\%, \ge 90\%, and 100\% [attack-free]) in the time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month during the Treatment Period compared to the Run-in Period.
- Time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month requiring on-demand therapy during the Treatment Period.
- Time-normalized number of moderate or severe HAE attacks per month during the Treatment Period.
L183: HAE attack severity was assessed by the investigator based on the subject's description of the attack and reflected the maximum intensity of the attack. The Applicant specified the following qualifiers for investigator determination of HAE attack severity in the clinical study protocol (dated August 4, 2020), though they could have varied between subjects and attacks:
L184: Mild:
L185: - Little to no effect on daily activities.
- Use of HAE on-demand therapy to treat the attack may not be necessary.
- Other concomitant medication (e.g., analgesics) may be used to treat attack symptoms.
L186: Moderate:
L187: - Causes daily activities to be difficult.
- Some assistance may be needed to complete daily activities.
- Use of HAE on-demand therapy to treat the attack is probable.
L188: ## Severe:
L189: - Causes marked limitation of daily activities.
- Medical assistance and intervention may be required, including at clinic emergency room visit or hospitalization.
- HAE on-demand therapy is used to treat the attack.
L190: An exploratory objective of this trial was to further evaluate the efficacy and quality of life associated with the use of garadacimab in subjects with C1-INH (Type I or Type II) HAE. This included analyzing an exploratory endpoint of change in subject total and domain scores from the angioedema quality of life (AE-QoL) questionnaire from baseline to Day 182 by treatment arm.
L191: ## 6.2.2. Eligibility Criteria, Trial 3001
L192: Key eligibility criteria are summarized below. The trial enrolled subjects \ge 12 years of age with clinically confirmed HAE and an attack frequency that would likely warrant prophylactic HAE therapy. The inclusion and exclusion criteria are acceptable and consistent with those used in previous studies of approved products for HAE prophylaxis.
L193: ## Key Inclusion Criteria
L194: - Male or female \ge 12 years of age at the time of informed consent.
- Diagnosed with clinically confirmed C1-INH (Type I or Type II) HAE:Documented clinical history consistent with HAE (SC or mucosal, nonpruritic swelling episodes without accompanying urticaria), and
- C1-INH antigen and/or functional activity \le 50\% of normal, and
- C4 antigen concentration below the lower limit of the reference range.
- Experienced \ge 3 HAE attacks during the 3 months before Screening (subjects taking any prophylactic HAE therapy prior to Screening could present documentation of \ge 3 HAE attacks during the three consecutive months before commencing the prophylactic therapy).
L195: ## Key Exclusion Criteria
L196: - Concomitant diagnosis of another form of angioedema, such as idiopathic or acquired angioedema, recurrent angioedema associated with urticaria, or FXII/PLG (Type III) HAE.
- For adult subjects: use of C1-INH products, androgens, antifibrinolytics, or other small molecule medications for routine prophylaxis against HAE attacks within 2 weeks prior to the Run-in Period.
- For adolescent subjects (12 to 17 years of age): use of long-term prophylactic therapy for HAE before Screening.
- Use of a monoclonal antibody, such as lanadelumab, within 3 months prior to the Run-in Period.
L197: ## 6.2.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trial 3001
L198: The final statistical analysis plan (statistical analysis plan (SAP); version 1.0) is dated April 21, 2021. For details about the sample size calculation for this trial, see Section 15.1.3. The following analysis sets were defined by the Applicant:
L199: **Screened Analysis Set**: All subjects who provided written informed consent (or assent for minors) and who underwent study screening procedures.
L200: **Intention-to-Treat Analysis Set**: All subjects in the Screened Analysis Set who were randomized. The intent-to-treat (ITT) Analysis Set was analyzed using the treatment to which the subject was randomized, regardless of the treatment actually received. The ITT Analysis Set was the main set used for efficacy evaluation.
L201: **Safety Analysis Set**: All subjects in the ITT Analysis Set who received at least one dose of investigational product. The Safety Analysis Set was analyzed using the actual treatment received and was the main set used for safety evaluation.
L202: **Per Protocol Analysis Set**: All subjects in the ITT Analysis Set who received at least one dose of investigational product and complied with the protocol. Decisions regarding exclusion from the Per Protocol Analysis Set were made and documented prior to unblinding.
L203: Use of the phrase “complied with the protocol” in the definition for the Per Protocol Analysis Set is vague, and the Applicant did not provide any further details. However, there was only one major protocol violation in the trial (one subject missed a visit and then withdrew consent).
L204: ## 6.2.3.1. Testing Hierarchy and Multiplicity Control, Trial 3001
L205: Four hypothesis tests (one for the primary endpoint [H01] and three for the key secondary endpoints [H02, H03, and H04]) were performed hierarchically at a two-sided alpha of 5%. Below is a description of the four null hypotheses associated with these four tests, per the Applicant’s final SAP:
L206: - H01: The time-normalized numbers of HAE attacks in the first 6-month time period of the Active Arm and in the Placebo Arm period are equal.
- H02: The percentage reductions in the mean time-normalized number of HAE attacks for the 6 months of the Active Arm compared to the 6-month Placebo Arm period are equal to zero.
- H03: The number of subjects who do not experience a HAE attack in the first 3 months of the Active Arm and the first 3-month Placebo Period are equal.
- H04: The percentage of subjects with *good* or *excellent* responses to the SGART at the end of the Treatment Period (Day 182) are equal for subjects treated with garadacimab and placebo.
L207: From the final SAP, the general flow of the hypothesis testing was as follows:
L208: The null hypothesis associated with the primary endpoint (H01) was tested first at a two-sided alpha of 5%. If and only if the resulting two-sided p-value was &lt;0.05, testing of H02 would be performed. If and only if H02 was rejected (with a two-sided p-value &lt;0.05), H03 would be tested. If and only if H03 was rejected (with a two-sided p-value &lt;0.05), H04 would be tested at a two-sided alpha of 5%.
L209: The definition for H02 was unclear in the trial protocol (dated August 4, 2020). Prior to database lock, the Applicant modified H02 in the final SAP, but it included two different specifications for H02. In the Applicant's response (dated June 27, 2024) to an FDA Information Request (IR), it was clarified that H02 was defined as written above. In a separate IR response (dated July 23, 2024), the Applicant also clarified that H02 was evaluating the percentage reduction in the means relative to placebo during the Treatment Period, not the percentage reduction in the means for each treatment arm relative to the Run-in Period (this was an additional secondary endpoint outside of the testing hierarchy). H01 and H02 were essentially evaluating different summary measures of the same endpoint; no rationale was provided for including H02 in the testing hierarchy. The Applicant may have wanted to evaluate the percentage reduction for increased clinical interpretability (since the results of a Wilcoxon rank sum test are not as straightforward) and labeling purposes.
L210: ## 6.2.3.2. Analysis of the Primary Endpoint, Trial 3001
L211: The Applicant's primary objective was to assess the treatment effect of garadacimab during the 6-month Treatment Period (from Day 1 [first study drug administration] through Day 182) while subjects were allowed to treat HAE attacks with on-demand therapy.
L212: ## Primary Estimand
L213: The Applicant defined the following attributes for the primary estimand:
L214: - Treatment Condition: Monthly treatment of garadacimab or placebo.
- Population: The target subject population defined by the study eligibility criteria and who were randomized (the ITT Analysis Set).
- Variable: Time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month during treatment from Day 1 through Day 182.
- Intercurrent Events: All observed values were used regardless of the occurrence of the following intercurrent events (treatment policy strategy):Administration of on-demand therapy in addition to prophylactic treatment with garadacimab.
- Prohibited concomitant medications due or not due to coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19) or COVID-19 vaccination.
- Noncompliance to treatment due or not due to COVID-19 or COVID-19 vaccination.
- Early study discontinuation due or not due to COVID-19.
L215: The intercurrent event of early study discontinuation due or not due to COVID-19 was removed prior to database lock per FDA feedback, as the Agency did not consider this to be an intercurrent event. In the final SAP, the Applicant prespecified that subjects who discontinue treatment or the study but are on study treatment for at least 30 days would be included in the primary analysis. Additionally, no subjects discontinued treatment or the study as a result of COVID-19.
L216: - Population-Level Summary: Median time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month during treatment from Day 1 through Day 182.
L217: ## **Primary Analysis**
L218: The analysis method for evaluating the primary endpoint in the ITT Analysis Set was a two-sided Wilcoxon rank sum test at \alpha=5\%. The population-level summary specified in the Applicant's primary estimand was the median, but the Wilcoxon rank sum test is not a median test.
L219: If a subject discontinued the study early after being in the Treatment Period for the first 30 days continuously, observation was terminated by the Early Termination Visit. Based on the data from the phase 2 trial (2001), it was assumed that the attack rate for subjects who discontinued the study after Study Day 30 and before the end of Month 6 would be comparable to the attack rate among subjects who completed the entire 6-month Treatment Period. Therefore, the primary endpoint was considered measurable and not missing for these subjects (the time-normalized number of HAE attacks was calculated based on the HAE attacks reported until the subject's Early Termination Visit).
L220: If a subject discontinued treatment due or not due to COVID-19 but continued participation in the study for more than 30 days beyond the last study drug administration, the HAE attacks until the last study drug administration plus 30 days were included in the calculation of the primary endpoint (the study drug was administered monthly). For these subjects, length of treatment (in days) was calculated as:
L221: [date of last study drug administration – date of Study Visit Day 1 of the Treatment Period + 31].
L222: ## **Missing Data**
L223: The final SAP prespecified that the primary endpoint (the time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month) would only be considered missing for subjects who discontinued study treatment within 30 days after the first study drug administration.
L224: ## **Sensitivity Analyses**
L225: The sensitivity analysis below was specified in the Applicant's final SAP.
L226: To evaluate the robustness of the primary efficacy results, the time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month was compared between the garadacimab and placebo arms using a generalized linear model for count data assuming a Poisson distribution with the log link function and Pearson chi-squared scaling of standard errors to account for potential overdispersion. The model included treatment arm and baseline attack rate (the time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month during the Run-in Period) as covariates. To account for differences in treatment duration among subjects, the logarithm of subject time on treatment was included as an offset term in the model.
L227: From this model, the least squares (LS) mean time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month and associated standard errors were estimated for the garadacimab and placebo arms. To report the mean HAE attack rate per month, the received model estimates were transformed by the exponential function and scaled by time. The corresponding attack ratio for garadacimab relative to placebo and its 95% CI were also reported. The estimated attack ratio was used to calculate the percentage difference in the means as follows: 100\% \times (\text{attack ratio} - 1). The 95% CI for the percentage difference in means was calculated using the upper and lower confidence limits obtained for the attack ratio, transformed by subtracting 1 and then multiplying by 100%.
L228: This sensitivity analysis was applied to the ITT Analysis Set.
L229: ## Subgroup Analyses
L230: In the final SAP, the Applicant prespecified that if five or more Japanese subjects were enrolled in the trial, then a descriptive analysis of the primary endpoint (the time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month) would be conducted for the subset of Japanese subjects (the Applicant did not provide a rationale for this analysis). Since a total of six Japanese subjects were enrolled in the trial, the Applicant completed this subgroup analysis and submitted the results for review. Analyses of the primary endpoint by other clinically relevant subgroups were not prespecified or submitted by the Applicant. Additional analyses of the primary endpoint by other clinically relevant subgroups (by age, sex, geographic location, HAE subtype, HAE attack location and severity, and other races/ethnicities) were completed by the Agency to inform regulatory decision-making. See Section 6.2.4.6 for the results of subgroup analyses for the primary endpoint.
L231: ## 6.2.3.3. Analysis of the Key Secondary Endpoints, Trial 3001
L232: The Applicant prespecified the following statistical tests for the three key secondary endpoints:
L233: - Percentage reduction in the means of the time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month was assessed using a two-sided Wilcoxon rank sum test.
- Number of subjects who did not experience an HAE attack (i.e., were attack-free) in the first 3 months of the 6-month Treatment Period was evaluated using a two-sided Fisher exact test.
- Percentage of subjects with *good* or *excellent* responses to the SGART at the end of the Treatment Period was assessed using a two-sided chi-squared test.
L234: The first key secondary endpoint above was defined as:
L235: 100 \times \left[ \frac{\text{mean time-normalized number of HAE attacks for garadacimab} - \text{mean time-normalized number of HAE attacks for placebo}}{\text{mean time-normalized number of HAE attacks for placebo}} \right]
L236: Its corresponding hypothesis (H02) was “the percentage reduction in means of the time-normalized number of HAE attacks for the 6 months of the Active Arm compared to the 6-month Placebo Arm period are equal to zero” (specified in the protocol, and confirmed by the Applicant’s IR responses dated June 27, 2024, and July 23, 2024). The Wilcoxon rank sum test is a nonparametric approach to test location shift between two distributions. However, H02 was to test whether a summary statistic equaled zero, for which the Wilcoxon rank-sum test would not be applicable. In Section 6.2.4.4, only descriptive statistics have been presented for this endpoint.
L237: Key secondary estimands were not defined by the Applicant.
L238: ## Missing Data
L239: Secondary endpoints based on HAE attack rate (e.g., the first key secondary endpoint) used the same missing data assumptions as the primary endpoint (see *Missing Data* in Section 6.2.3.2).
L240: ## 6.2.3.4. Other Secondary/Exploratory Endpoint Analyses, Trial 3001
L241: Additional secondary and exploratory endpoints in this trial included:
L242: - Percentage reduction (\ge 50\%, \ge 70\%, \ge 90\% and 100% [attack-free]) in the time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month during the Treatment Period compared to the Run-in Period.
- Time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month requiring on-demand therapy during the Treatment Period.
- Time-normalized number of moderate or severe HAE attacks per month during the Treatment Period.
- Change from baseline to Day 182 in total and domain scores from the AE-QoL questionnaire.
L243: Percentage reductions were assessed descriptively by treatment arm, and an exploratory, two-sided Fisher exact test was prespecified and conducted for subjects with a 100% reduction only. The time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month requiring on-demand therapy and the time-normalized number of moderate or severe HAE attacks per month were evaluated by treatment arm using exploratory, two-sided Wilcoxon rank-sum tests. The Agency was also interested in assessing these two endpoints using exploratory Poisson models structured similarly to the Applicant's sensitivity analysis for the primary endpoint described in Section 6.2.3.2.
L244: AE-QoL data were collected only from subjects 18 years of age or older and were summarized descriptively by treatment arm. AE-QoL total and domain scores were calculated as:
L245: 100 \times [\text{sum of all completed items} \div \text{maximum possible sum of all completed items}]
L246: Estimands were not defined for any of the endpoints above.
L247: ## Missing Data
L248: Additional secondary and exploratory endpoints based on HAE attack rate used the same missing data assumptions as the primary endpoint (see *Missing Data* in Section 6.2.3.2).
L249: For the AE-QoL data, domain scores were not calculated if more than one item was missing for a domain, and total scores were not calculated if more than four items were missing in total. Subjects without an AE-QoL domain and/or total score were excluded from the descriptive summary accordingly.
L250: ## 6.2.4. Results of Analyses, Trial 3001
L251: This section summarizes subject disposition, baseline demographics and clinical characteristics, and the primary, secondary, and exploratory efficacy results for the endpoints listed in Section 6.2.1. The results of additional analyses of interest to the Agency are also reported.
L252: ## 6.2.4.1. Subject Disposition, Trial 3001
L253: Disposition information is provided in Table 8. A total of 80 subjects were screened, of which 4 were screen failures due to not obtaining informed consent (N=1), not having a clinically
L254: confirmed diagnosis of C1-INH (Type I or Type II) HAE (N=2), or not experiencing \ge 3 HAE attacks during the 3 months before screening (N=1). Of the 76 subjects who entered the Runin- Period, 11 were not eligible to advance to the Treatment Period (most due to &lt;1 month of participation in the Runin- Period, an insufficient number of HAE attacks experienced during the Runin- Period, or clinically significant laboratory abnormalities).

L261: Overall, the subject disposition in this trial is reasonable for an HAE program.
L262: ## 6.2.4.2. Baseline Demographics and Clinical Characteristics,
## Trial 3001
L263: The demographics and clinical characteristics of the ITT Set at baseline are summarized in **Table 9** and were appreciably similar between the treatment arms for this small trial (N=64). The
L264: study enrolled more females (59%) than males (41%), which is generally consistent with the enrollment patterns seen in previous HAE programs. A total of six adolescents (9% of the ITT set) were enrolled, of which four were randomized to garadacimab and two to placebo (this is considered adequate, as the pathophysiology of C1-INH [Type I or Type II] HAE is similar across all age groups). Racial and ethnic group representation in this trial is congruent with the overall subject population. Most of the ITT Set had Type I HAE (88%) and a history of laryngeal HAE attacks (59%). Enrollment occurred at sites in the United States (33% of the ITT Set) and outside the United States (67% of the ITT Set). Additionally, 33% of the ITT Set reported use of HAE prophylaxis therapy during the 3 months prior to Screening, 89% had a family history of HAE, and the average HAE duration was 22 years.

L270: ## 6.2.4.3. Primary Efficacy Results, Trial 3001
L271: The primary efficacy endpoint was the time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month during the 6-month Treatment Period. This endpoint is clinically meaningful and consistent with previous programs for HAE prophylaxis. Details regarding the reporting, collection, and investigator confirmation of HAE attacks are provided in Section 15.1.1.
L272: The primary efficacy results are summarized in Table 10; the total number of HAE attacks in the garadacimab arm was over fourfold lower than in the placebo arm (63 versus 264). The estimated mean time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month was considerably lower in the garadacimab arm (mean=0.27; 95% CI=0.05, 0.49) compared to placebo (mean=2.01; 95% CI=1.44, 2.57), signifying an improvement in the garadacimab arm over placebo. Furthermore, there was a statistically significant difference (Wilcoxon p-value &lt;0.001) in the rank sums of the time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month in the garadacimab and placebo arms (i.e., the rank sum in the garadacimab arm was significantly lower than the placebo arm, assuming that a higher rank indicates a worse outcome).

L279: Missing primary endpoint data only occurred for one subject (4%) on placebo who received less than 30 days of study treatment. Therefore, the impact of the Applicant's missing data approach was minimal, and the primary efficacy results were robust.
L280: Robustness of the primary analysis results is also supported by the Applicant's Poisson model described under *Sensitivity Analyses* in Section 6.2.3.2. After adjusting for the baseline attack rate, the estimated mean time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month was considerably
L281: lower in the garadacimab arm (LS mean=0.22, 95% CI=0.1, 0.45) compared to placebo (LS mean=2.01, 95% CI=1.45, 2.79), similar to the primary analysis (Table 10), with a percentage reduction in the means of 89.2% (95% CI=75.6%, 95.2%) relative to placebo. Additionally, the estimated attack ratio relative to placebo was 0.11 (95% CI=0.05, 0.24). The above results are shown in Table 11 and further signify an improvement in the garadacimab arm over placebo.

L289: ## 6.2.4.4. Key Secondary Efficacy Results, Trial 3001
L290: The first key secondary endpoint in the testing hierarchy (see Section 6.2.3.1) was the percentage reduction in the means of the time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month during the Treatment Period. H02 was specified to test “the percentage reduction in means of the time-normalized number of HAE attacks for the 6-months of the Active Arm compared to the 6-month Placebo Arm period are equal to zero.” The mean time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month during the 6-month Treatment Period was 0.27 for the garadacimab arm and 2.01 for the placebo arm (Table 10). From the Clinical Study Report (dated October 6, 2022), the percentage reduction in the mean relative to placebo was 86.5% (95% CI=57.8%, 95.7%).
L291: As mentioned in Section 6.2.3.3, the Wilcoxon rank sum test is a nonparametric approach to test location shift between two distributions and is not applicable to test H02 for “the percentage reduction in means of the time-normalized number of HAE attacks for the 6-months of the Active Arm compared to the 6-month Placebo Arm period are equal to zero.” Furthermore, from the Applicant-submitted software program for analysis of this endpoint, the p-value from the Wilcoxon rank sum test for the primary endpoint was reported as the p-value for this endpoint, which is not appropriate. Therefore, in this review, no p-value is reported for this endpoint.
L292: The first key secondary endpoint and the primary endpoint use different descriptive summary measures (the median for the primary endpoint and the percentage reduction in the means for the first key secondary endpoint) to evaluate the same endpoint (the time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month for the 6-month Treatment Period). The first key secondary endpoint does not provide any new information for evidence of effectiveness of the study drug.
L293: The second key secondary endpoint (H03) was the number of subjects who experienced zero HAE attacks (i.e., were attack-free) in the first 3 months of the 6-month Treatment Period. A total of 30/63 evaluable subjects (48%) did not experience any HAE attacks during the first 3 months of the Treatment Period, of which 28 were on garadacimab (72% of the garadacimab arm; 95% Wilson CI=56%, 83%) and 2 were on placebo (8% of the placebo arm; 95% Wilson CI=2%, 26%), as shown in Table 12. In addition, there was a statistically significant association between treatment arm and the number of attack-free subjects during the first 3 months of the Treatment Period (Fisher p-value &lt;0.001).

L303: The last key secondary endpoint (H04) was the percentage of subjects with SGART responses of *good* or *excellent* at the end of the 6-month Treatment Period. As shown in Table 13, a total of 39/62 evaluable subjects (63%) provided responses of *good* or *excellent*, of which 31 were on garadacimab (82% of the garadacimab arm) and 8 were on placebo (33% of the placebo arm). Furthermore, there was a statistically significant association between treatment arm and the number of subjects with SGART responses of *good* or *excellent* at the end of the Treatment Period (chi-squared p-value &lt;0.001). For more details regarding the SGART and its utility in this HAE setting, see Section 6.3.1.

L313: ## 6.2.4.5. Additional Efficacy Results, Trial 3001
L314: ## Secondary Endpoints
L315: Other clinically meaningful, prespecified secondary endpoints were also analyzed to further examine the effectiveness of garadacimab for the proposed indication of prophylaxis of HAE attacks. These prespecified secondary endpoints included:
L316: - Proportion of subjects with a \ge 50\%, \ge 70\%, \ge 90\%, and 100% reduction in the time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month during the 6-month Treatment Period compared to the Run-in Period.
- Time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month requiring on-demand therapy during the 6-month Treatment Period.
- Time-normalized number of moderate or severe HAE attacks per month during the 6-month Treatment Period.
L317: As shown in Table 14, the proportions of subjects with a \ge 50\%, \ge 70\%, \ge 90\%, and 100% reductions in monthly HAE attacks were 95%, 92%, 74%, and 62% on garadacimab and 33%, 17%, 8%, and 0% on placebo, respectively. These results provide further evidence supporting the efficacy of garadacimab.

L326: The Applicant also assessed the time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month requiring on-demand therapy during the 6-month Treatment Period. A total of 54/63 (86%) of the HAE attacks experienced in the garadacimab arm during the Treatment Period required on-demand therapy, while a slightly higher proportion of 245/264 (93%) of the HAE attacks experienced in the placebo arm required on-demand therapy. As shown in **Table 15**, the estimated mean time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month requiring on-demand therapy was considerably lower in the garadacimab arm (mean=0.23; 95% CI=0.02, 0.45) compared to placebo (mean=1.86; 95% CI=1.26, 2.46). These exploratory results also favored garadacimab.

L367: The exploratory efficacy results presented above also favored garadacimab.
L368: ## 6.2.4.6. Subgroup Analyses, Trial 3001
L369: Garadacimab met its prespecified primary efficacy endpoint of time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month (see Section 6.2.4.3) in adults and adolescents with C1-INH (Type I or Type II) HAE in trial 3001. However, when evaluating the efficacy of garadacimab, we also considered whether the primary endpoint was consistent across clinically relevant subgroups that were not considered by the Applicant, including by sex, age, race, ethnicity, geographic location, HAE subtype, and HAE attack location and severity.
L370: ## Efficacy by Sex
L371: For both males and females, there was a clinically meaningful and statistically persuasive reduction in the mean time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month relative to placebo (Figure 4). These results are similar to the overall primary analysis results (Table 10).
L372: **Figure 4. Subgroup Analysis for the Primary Endpoint by Sex (ITT Analysis Set), Trial 3001**

L376: ## **Efficacy by Age**
L377: The primary efficacy endpoint was further analyzed by age subgroups (adolescents 12 to &lt;18 years of age, adults 18 to &lt;65 years of age, and geriatrics \ge65 years of age). For adults, there was a clinically meaningful and statistically persuasive reduction in the mean time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month relative to placebo (Figure 5), which aligns with the overall primary analysis results (Table 10). Although the point estimate favored garadacimab in the adolescent subpopulation, the CI is very wide given the small number of adolescent subjects enrolled (N=6). The observed treatment effect in adolescents may have also been less robust because subjects had less disease severity, as the trial eligibility criteria did not allow prior use of prophylactic therapies for adolescent subjects.
L378: **Figure 5. Subgroup Analysis for the Primary Endpoint by Age Group (ITT Analysis Set), Trial 3001**

L381: Abbreviations: CI, confidence interval; HAE, hereditary angioedema; ITT, intent-to-treat; N, number of evaluable subjects in the ITT Analysis Set; n, number of subjects in the respective category
L382: Conclusions regarding the effectiveness of garadacimab in adolescents primarily relied on matching PK exposure to adults and extrapolating efficacy from the adult population, as the pathophysiology of HAE is the same across all age groups. Extrapolation of the efficacy of garadacimab in the adolescent population was based on matching of the PK profile and the mean garadacimab concentration-time profile to the adult population (see Section 15.2.5 for further details). While the pivotal trial (3001) was not statistically powered to draw efficacy conclusions in the adolescent subpopulation, it is reassuring that the primary efficacy endpoint in adolescents trended in the same direction as adults (Figure 5).
L383: Additionally, there were six geriatric subjects in trial 3001, all of whom were randomized to garadacimab. For the geriatric subjects, the mean time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month was 0.03 (95% CI=0, 0.1), which is a meaningful reduction compared to the overall placebo group (Table 10).
L384: Altogether, garadacimab appeared effective at reducing HAE attacks during the 6-month Treatment Period irrespective of age.
L385: ## **Efficacy by Race and Ethnicity**
L386: The point estimates for the primary analysis by race subgroups that comprised more than one subject per arm (Figure 6) were generally consistent with the overall study population (Table 10). However, due to the small number of Asian (Japanese) subjects enrolled in the trial (N=6), substantive conclusions for this subgroup cannot be drawn.

L392: With regard to ethnicity, there were three subjects who were Hispanic or Latino, one on garadacimab with a monthly HAE attack rate of 0.17 and two on placebo with monthly HAE attack rates of 0.17 and 1.27.
L393: Given the small number of subjects who self-identified as non-white in the trial, it is difficult to draw definitive conclusions regarding efficacy by race/ethnicity; however, the data do not raise substantial concerns regarding a differential treatment effect.

L395: The primary efficacy endpoint was also evaluated by geographic location (Figure 7). For subjects at U.S. and ex-U.S. sites, there was a clinically meaningful and statistically persuasive reduction in the mean time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month relative to placebo. While the results for the ex-U.S. subgroup aligned more with the overall primary analysis results (Table 10) than those for the U.S. subgroup, both trended in the same direction favoring garadacimab.

While similar conclusions can be drawn for attack severity, a higher number of mild abdominal attacks were experienced in the garadacimab arm compared to placebo (20 versus 14), and two subjects in each treatment arm experienced a severe laryngeal attack.
