L420: During the clinical site inspection, the Office of Regulatory Affairs inspector noted some discrepancies in the HAE attack location recorded on the case report form (CRF) and the line
L421: listing provided in the clinical study report (CSR). In some instances, the CSR did not reflect all locations involved in an HAE attack that were recorded in the CRF. For example, the CRF might have documented the attack location as “eyelids bilaterally, buttock (right), left arm, right arm, abdomen,” but the CSR line listing only contained the terms “cutaneous, extremities,” omitting “abdomen.” This discrepancy may affect the accuracy of the subgroup analyses by location but did not affect the primary efficacy endpoint, as the overall number of HAE attacks reported in the source documents matched the number reported by the Applicant in the CSR. See Section 23 for additional details.
L422: Overall, the results reported above align with the primary analysis results and support the effectiveness of garadacimab in reducing HAE attacks, irrespective of attack location and severity.
L423: ## Conclusion
L424: While the interpretation of the subgroup analyses above is limited by small sample sizes, the treatment benefit of garadacimab based on the primary endpoint appears to be similar regardless of sex, age, race, ethnicity, geographic location, HAE subtype, and HAE attack location and severity. Furthermore, due to the pathophysiology of HAE being the same across all age groups, extrapolation of the adult efficacy data to adolescents is clinically reasonable and supported by PK matching (see Section 15.2.5).
L425: ## 6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
L426: ## 6.3.1. Patient-Reported Outcome Endpoints
L427: ## Issue
L428: Determine whether the SGART and the AE-QoL questionnaires are fit-for-purpose for this context of use to support regulatory decision-making (b) (4).
L429: ## Background
L430: HAE is characterized by unpredictable, recurrent episodes of angioedema, which greatly impact quality of life and daily functioning. At the HAE Patient-Focused Drug Development meeting held at the Agency on September 25, 20171, patients with HAE shared the aspects of the disease that had the most impact on their lives, including the unpredictability of attack occurrence, the painful abdominal attacks, the dramatic change swelling had on their physical appearance, and the emotional toll the disease had on their lives.
L431: The SGART and AE-QoL were used to derive secondary and exploratory endpoints, respectively, in trial 3001, (b) (4). There are currently no fit-for-purpose patient-reported outcomes (PROs) for use in HAE prophylaxis clinical trials.
L432: 1 https://www.fda.gov/files/about%20fda/published/The-Voice-of-the-Patient---Hereditary-Angioedema.pdf.
L433: ## **Assessment**
L434: The Applicant did not request Agency feedback on the SGART or AE-QoL endpoints during development, nor did they provide exact copies of the SGART and AE-QoL that they administered during the study (e.g., electronic screen shots) or any associated training materials or user manuals for review. The Applicant did not provide a PRO evidence dossier to support the measurement properties of the SGART and AE-QoL and to establish their fitness-for-purpose for the context of use. While existing literature demonstrate acceptable psychometric properties of the AE-QoL (Weller et al. 2012; Weller et al. 2016), this will not replace or rectify problems with its content validity. In general, the content validity of a clinical outcome assessment needs to be established prior to establishing its quantitative psychometric properties.
L435: ## **SGART**
L436: The SGART is a self-reported instrument completed electronically via a provisioned electronic device (e.g., a tablet) at the study site to measure a subject's overall treatment response to the investigational product based on the question: "Considering all of the ways HAE affects you, please rate your response to the study medication you were given to prevent HAE attacks during this Treatment Period." Specifically, subjects of all ages responded to the single-item question using a 5-point verbal rating scale on Days 91 and 182, respectively. The five response options were:
L437: - (0) None – worse or no response at all, not acceptable.
- (1) Poor – very little response, not acceptable.
- (2) Fair – some response, acceptable but could be better.
- (3) Good – good response, acceptable.
- (4) Excellent – excellent response, as good as can be imagined.
L438: To construct the key secondary endpoint, the SGART asks subjects to respond based on their experience in the past 6 months, making it susceptible to recall error. A global impression score may be influenced by factors outside of the disease process, is unable to characterize the drug's effect on individual signs/symptoms, and may mask the fact that an overall improvement in score can be achieved by improvement in some signs/symptoms with concurrent worsening in other signs/symptoms. In addition, the SGART appears to assess some aspect of treatment satisfaction based on the phrasing of its item and response options. From a regulatory perspective, it is more appropriate to assess whether subjects experienced a meaningful improvement in symptoms rather than satisfaction with treatment. Although the percentage of subjects with good/excellent SGART responses at the end of the 6-month Treatment Period was a prespecified secondary endpoint in the testing hierarchy and achieved statistically significant results compared to placebo, the SGART does not appear to be content-valid for the context of use (b) (4)
L439: ## **AE-QoL**
L440: The AE-QoL questionnaire was developed to assess quality of life impairment in recurrent angioedema patients, including patients with HAE (Weller et al. 2012). It is a 17-item instrument that uses a 5-point Likert scale with response options of never, rarely, sometimes, often, and very
L441: often as well as a recall period of the last four weeks. The 17 items within the AE-QoL are organized into four distinct domains labeled as follows: functioning, fatigue/mood, fears/shame, and food/nutrition (Table 21).
L442: **Table 21. Conceptual Framework of the AE-QoL Questionnaire**
L443: Item	Domain	General Concept
Item 1: Restrictions in work because of swelling episodes	Functioning	Functioning limitations associated with swelling episodes
Item 2: Restrictions in physical activity because of swelling episodes	
Item 3: Restrictions in leisure time because of swelling episodes	
Item 4: Restrictions in social relations because of swelling episodes	
Item 6: Difficulty falling asleep	Fatigue/mood	Fatigue or mood difficulties associated with swelling episodes
Item 7: Waking up during the night	
Item 8: Being tired during the day	
Item 9: Difficulty concentrating	
Item 10: Feeling depressed	
Item 12: Burden associated with swelling episodes	Fears/shame	Fears or shame associated with swelling episodes
Item 13: Afraid of swelling episodes that occur suddenly	
Item 14: Afraid of increased frequency of swelling episodes	
Item 15: Ashamed to go out in public because of swelling episodes	
Item 16: Feeling embarrassed or self-conscious because of swelling episodes	
Item 17: Afraid of negative long-term treatment effects	
Item 5: Restrictions in eating and drinking because of swelling episodes	Food	Food limitations associated with swelling episodes
Item 11: Limited food or beverage choices because of swelling episodes
L444: Source: Division of Clinical Outcome Assessment Reviewer.
L445: Abbreviations, AE-QoL, Angioedema-Quality of Life Questionnaire
L446: Each domain has its own score, and a total score can also be calculated. The items are scored from 0 to 4, and they are summed to provide raw domain scores, which are then transformed into linear scores ranging from 0 to 100. The total score is the sum of all 17 items. Higher scores in the AE-QoL domain and total scores indicate greater levels of impairment of quality of life. In trial 3001, subjects \ge 18 years were asked to complete the AE-QoL via a provisioned electronic device at the trial site on Days 1, 31, 61, 91, 121, 151, and 182, respectively.
L447: There is inadequate qualitative evidence in Weller et al. (2012) to support the content validity of the AE-QoL for use in the Applicant's drug development program. In addition, the domains of the AE-QoL are not well-defined and reliable to inform regulatory decision-making. Specifically, for the AE-QoL functioning domain, we note that asking patients about restrictions
L448: swelling episodes have on their daily lives regardless of whether or not they have actually experienced swelling episodes during a 4-week period will not provide useful information for assessing treatment benefit in the context of trial 3001. While the core concepts of disease-related symptoms and physical function are reasonable PRO measurement strategies for demonstrating treatment benefit, emotional impacts (such as anxiety and embarrassment) as assessed by the AE-QoL fears/shame domain are distal concepts that may be affected by factors external to HAE disease or effect of the investigational product. In addition, we do not agree that the 4-week recall period of the AE-QoL is appropriate as patients may not reliably recall the occurrence of their HAE attacks during this timeframe. As such, we do not believe that the AE-QoL is content valid for the context of use.
L449: The Applicant proposed a 6-point change as a minimal clinically important difference in the AE-QoL total and domain scores, positioned as exploratory endpoints in trial 3001. In general, endpoints positioned as exploratory endpoints and not multiplicity-controlled are (b) (4). The Applicant did not conduct anchor-based analyses to interpret meaningful within-patient score changes in PRO endpoints scores in trial 3001 as no external anchor scales (e.g., a Patient Global Impression of Severity or Patient Global Impression of Change) were included in trial 3001. Instead, the Applicant referenced the 6-point minimal clinically important difference threshold reported in Weller et al. (2016). Given the issues with the content validity of the AE-QoL, it is premature to evaluate meaningful score changes for the AE-QoL. While an anchor-based method is the primary method for deriving a range of thresholds that would constitute a meaningful within-patient change in target PRO scores, it should be supplemented with empirical cumulative distribution function and probability density function curves. In addition, estimation of clinically meaningful within-patient change thresholds should be confirmed in the specific context of the drug development trial. Detailed recommendations on the conduct of anchor-based analyses, including properties of appropriate anchor scales, can be found in FDA's Patient-Focused Drug Development Discussion Document: Incorporating Clinical Outcome Assessments into Endpoints for Regulatory Decision-Making.2
L450: ## Conclusion
L451: Based on the evidence available to date and the construction of the instruments, the SGART and AE-QoL are not well-defined and reliable to inform regulatory decision making and thus, not fit-for-purpose (b) (4).
L452: ## 6.3.2. Type III HAE
L453: ## Issue
L454: Determine if there is sufficient evidence to conclude that garadacimab is effective in FXII/PLG (Type III) HAE patients (or a subset of Type III HAE patients) for the purposes of labeling.
L455: ## Background
L456: HAE is a rare autosomal disease classified into three subtypes: I, II, and III. HAE is most commonly caused by variants in the *SERPING1* gene, resulting in low or absent levels of C1-INH (Type I, most common) or normal to elevated levels of dysfunctional C1-INH (Type II).
L457: 2 https://www.fda.gov/media/166830/download
L458: However, HAE can also present with normal C1-INH (Type III) resulting from variants in other genes (e.g., *F12*, *PLG*, *ANGPT1*, *KNGL*, *MYOF*, *HS3ST6*); Type III HAE is a very rare, heterogeneous subtype (Santacroce et al. 2021). Genetic variants in *PLG* and factor XII (*F12*) are the most commonly reported variants in patients with Type III HAE (Bork et al. 2018). In addition to genetic differences, the *F12* and *PLG* variants also translate to differences in clinical presentation. For example, estrogen has a markedly aggravating effect on angioedema in *F12* HAE, and there is a higher frequency of tongue swelling in *PLG* HAE3. Erythema marginatum is occasionally observed in Type I and Type II HAE, but has not been reported for Type III HAE. Although the three types of HAE share the clinical presentation of episodic angioedema, Type III HAE exhibits some clinical phenotypic differences, such as predominant face/tongue swelling and less frequent abdominal attacks, when compared to Type I and Type II HAE.
L459: Garadacimab is a monoclonal antibody targeting the catalytic domain of factor XIIa, blocking its activation of the kallikrein-kinin system and in turn preventing the proteolytic conversion of high-molecular-weight kininogen to bradykinin. In Type I and Type II HAE, excess bradykinin generation has been shown to be the primary mechanism leading to angioedema. In Type III HAE, the mutations seem to affect the fibrinolytic and kallikrein-kinin system pathways at different levels which ultimately lead to bradykinin-mediated angioedema; however, the different gene mutations lead to different biochemical disturbances, resulting in different clinical features, which has led some authors to suggest that each Type III subtype be considered a distinct disease entity (Bork et al. 2020).
L460: Currently available HAE products are approved for patients with HAE, but historically clinical trials have only evaluated subjects with Type I and Type II HAE (due to both the rarity of and challenges in diagnosing Type III HAE), a fact which is reflected in the descriptions of the study populations in the product labeling. Although trial 3001 enrolled only subjects with Type I and Type II HAE, available response data from the subgroup of subjects with Type III HAE enrolled in the phase 2 trial (2001) are available for evaluation.
L461: ## Assessment
L462: The Applicant enrolled six subjects with Type III HAE in the phase 2 trial (2001). All six Type III HAE subjects received open-label garadacimab 600 mg SC Q4W (with a 300 mg IV loading dose). The Type III HAE subjects did not have a comparator arm for efficacy or safety assessments in trial 2001. Of the six Type III HAE subjects, three subjects had variants in *PLG*, and three had variants in *F12*. According to an IR response dated April 9, 2024, the three subjects with *PLG* variants each had the gain-of-function variant Lys330Glu, and the three subjects with variants in *F12* included Thr328Lys, deletion of 72 base pairs (c.971_1018 + 24del72), or duplication of 18 base pairs (c.892-909dup). Unlike Type I and Type II HAE subjects enrolled in trial 2001 who received multiple dosing regimens, subjects with Type III HAE were dosed at the highest garadacimab dose level studied (600 mg Q4W SC) throughout Treatment Periods 1 and 2 (Figure 65). The three subjects with variants in *PLG* exhibited less than a 50% reduction from baseline in HAE attacks; however, one subject experienced a decrease from three HAE attacks per month during the Run-in Period to 1.67 attacks per month while on garadacimab treatment. Two of the three subjects with *F12* variants responded to garadacimab (\ge50% reduction in HAE attacks) and enrolled in the phase 3b OLE study (3002).
L463: 3 https://ojrd.biomedcentral.com/counter/pdf/10.1186/s13023-020-01570-x.pdf.
L464: One subject with a *F12* variant experienced two HAE attacks per month during the Run-in Period and 2.33 attacks per month while on garadacimab treatment. In comparison, the Type I and Type II subjects in the 75 mg, 200 mg, and 400 mg garadacimab arms had reductions of \ge 50\%. Most Type I and Type II subjects (86%) in the 600 mg garadacimab arm also met this endpoint. No Type I or Type II subject on placebo achieved a \ge 50\% reduction. Refer to Sections 15.3 and 16.2 for more details.
L465: Although the number of subjects with *PLG* variants studied was small, the lack of response to garadacimab in some subjects with *PLG* variants suggests that the development of HAE attacks in these subjects may occur by a mechanism that bypasses factor XIIa, the target of garadacimab. This is supported by a report that *PLG* gain-of-function variants may result in the formation of bradykinin via downstream mechanisms (Hintze et al. 2022). Additionally, in vivo studies have shown that the incubation of *PLG* gain-of-function protein enhanced the breakdown of high-molecular-weight kininogen as compared to wild-type *PLG* protein, thereby increasing the formation of bradykinin (Maas 2019).
L466: ## Conclusion
L467: The garadacimab development program studied a small number of subjects with FXII-HAE (N=3) and PLG-HAE (N=3) in a phase 2 trial (2001) at a dosage higher than that included in the approved labeling. Of the subjects studied, 2/3 subjects with FXII-HAE and 0/3 with PLG-HAE responded to garadacimab (defined as a \ge 50\% reduction from baseline in HAE attacks). Subjects with *F12* and *PLG* mutations had variable responses to garadacimab treatment that could potentially be explained by emerging mechanistic data from in vivo studies. Given the extremely small sample size, lack of a control arm, and heterogeneity in disease genotype and phenotype, it is difficult to draw conclusions regarding the response to garadacimab treatment in the subset of subjects with Type III HAE. Therefore, no statement regarding patients with Type III HAE will be included in the PI. A broad HAE indication is reasonable for garadacimab, provided that language in the labeling describes that approval is supported only by results in subjects with Type I and Type II HAE. This approach is consistent with prior approvals and labeling for HAE prophylactic therapies. Further studies of whether subsets of subjects with Type III HAE respond to FXIIa inhibition with garadacimab, particularly PLG-HAE, are needed. Given that HAE patients typically receive healthcare from subspecialists with expertise in angioedema, academic research in the published literature may be the most efficient method for acquiring and disseminating information about differential treatment effects of garadacimab and other approved HAE products in patients with Type III HAE with specific mutations.
L468: ## 7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
L469: ## 7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Nonclinical Data
L470: Prolongation of aPTT was observed in nonclinical studies conducted with multiple nonclinical test species (e.g., monkeys, rabbits). There was no evidence of bleeding events in these nonclinical studies.
L471: ## 7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
L472: Garadacimab is a first-in-class inhibitor of activated FXIIa. FXIIa initiates the contact system and the intrinsic blood coagulation system. Based on its mechanism of action, potential risks include bleeding, thromboembolism, and abnormal coagulation laboratory values; however, patients with genetic FXII deficiency do not typically exhibit a bleeding tendency (see Section 7.7.1 for a discussion). These risks were monitored during the clinical trial and will be discussed in this review.
L473: ## 7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L474: Not applicable. This drug is not yet commercially available in any country.

L481: The clinical data scientists and clinical reviewer performed independent safety analyses using R Studio 4.1.3, JMP, MAED, and Office of Computational Science Analysis Studio software programs. L482: ## 7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
L483: No data quality or integrity issues were identified in the submitted datasets or CRFs. Definitions of adverse events (AEs) and serious adverse events (SAEs) were consistent with Code of Federal Regulations 312.32. The Applicant coded AEs using the Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities version 25.1 and determined AE severity using the mild, moderate, and severe grading scale (see Section 15.1.7 for definitions). The Applicant's translation of verbatim terms to the Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities preferred terms (PTs) for the events reported in the trial was reviewed and found to be acceptable. Safety monitoring in the clinical program was adequate to inform the risks of garadacimab. See Sections 15.1.6, 15.2.2, and 15.3.2 for the schedules and review of safety assessments.
L484: ## Overall Exposure
L485: Table 23 represents exposure to the proposed 200 mg dose of garadacimab in the HAE development program. Across the three trials, 172 subjects with HAE received at least one 200 mg dose of garadacimab, and there was &gt;1 year of exposure to garadacimab in the majority of subjects. Overall, the safety database is of sufficient size and duration for a rare disease such as HAE to assess the safety of garadacimab for the proposed indication.

L501: ## 7.6.1.2. Deaths, Trial 3001
L502: There were no deaths in trial 3001.
L503: ## 7.6.1.3. Serious Adverse Events, Trial 3001
L504: In trial 3001, there was one SAE. A 31-year-old female with Type I HAE in the garadacimab treatment group experienced a severe HAE laryngeal attack on Study Day 99, resulting in hospitalization. The subject reported sudden onset of globus sensation and difficulty swallowing. She self-treated with 3,000 IU of C1-INH and received icatibant 30 mg in the emergency
L505: department. No further on-demand treatment was administered during the hospitalization and her vitals remained stable. She was discharged from the hospital the following day and continued to receive study drug without interruption. She completed trial 3001 and continued treatment in OLE study 3002. As many HAE patients continue to experience breakthrough attacks despite prophylaxis therapy, this event is likely due to the underlying condition rather than an adverse reaction related to garadacimab treatment.
L506: ## 7.6.1.4. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events Leading to Treatment Discontinuation, Trial 3001
L507: There were no TEAEs that led to treatment discontinuation in trial 3001.
L508: ## 7.6.1.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Trial 3001

None of the infections appear to have been complicated as all resolved following treatment with oral antibiotics. None of the events led to garadacimab treatment discontinuation. Although there is an imbalance in the number of bacterial infections, the lack of consistent pattern to the type/anatomical location of infection or presumptive causative microorganism makes it difficult to draw conclusions regarding drug causality. Overall, considering the lack of a consistent microorganism or pattern of infection, that none of the infections were serious or severe in nature, and the lack of a plausible mechanism of action, this imbalance does not significantly alter the benefit/risk assessment, and this FMQ term does not warrant inclusion in labeling.

There were no concerning safety signals that significantly altered the risk of garadacimab in the HAE population.
L519: Due to the small sample size, a cutoff of 7% (or 3) subjects in the garadacimab arm reporting the same or similar TEAE was used as the threshold for inclusion in the draft labeling.

L527: ## 7.6.1.6. Adverse Events of Special Interest, Trial 3001
L528: Prespecified AEs of special interest (AESIs) included thromboembolic events, bleeding events, severe hypersensitivity reactions and anaphylaxis. Analyses of AEs by PT, Standard Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities Query (SMQ), and FMQ revealed no severe or life-threatening/serious AESIs or AESIs that led to treatment discontinuation. The individual AESIs are discussed in more detail under key safety review issues (Section 7.7).
L529: ## 7.6.1.7. Laboratory Values and Vital Signs, Trial 3001
L530: Routine clinical testing (hematology, serum chemistry with liver function tests, and coagulation) were collected at baseline, Month 3, and Month 6 during the Treatment Period. The results of the coagulation studies are discussed in *Review Issues* in Section 7.7.1. Otherwise, there was no significant clinical difference between the garadacimab and placebo groups in laboratory parameters during the Treatment Period. Additionally, no clinically meaningful changes in mean or median vital signs (systolic blood pressure, diastolic blood pressure, heart rate, respiratory rate, temperature) were observed with garadacimab treatment compared to placebo across trial visits.
L531: ## 7.6.1.8. Liver Function Tests, Trial 3001
L532: Liver function tests including alanine aminotransferase (ALT), aspartate aminotransferase (AST), alkaline phosphatase (ALP), and total bilirubin were collected from subjects at baseline, Month 3 and Month 6 during the 6-month Treatment Period. Subjects had an additional lab collection on Day 1 if baseline labs were collected more than 90 days prior to the first dose of garadacimab administration. There were no potential Hy's law cases in the program. One subject in the garadacimab treatment group was noted to have isolated elevations of ALP; the values were &lt;3\times upper limit of normal (ULN). None of the AST/ALT or ALP or total bilirubin values exceeded 3\times ULN. No significant difference in ALT, AST, ALP, and total bilirubin were found between the garadacimab and placebo groups.
L533: ## 7.6.1.9. Safety Subgroup Analyses Trial 3001
L534: Subgroup analyses by age, sex, race, and geographic region revealed no major differences in safety; however, the number of subjects within certain subgroups was small. Detailed analyses of TEAEs by subgroup are located in Section 18.
L535: ## 7.6.2. Safety Results, Study 3002
L536: ## 7.6.2.1. Study Design, Study 3002
L537: Study 3002 is an ongoing phase 3b, single arm, OLE study evaluating the long-term safety of garadacimab 200 mg SC every month in subjects with Types I, II, and III HAE. The trial enrolled rollover subjects who completed trial 2001 or 3001 as well as garadacimab-naïve subjects. For additional details regarding screening and the Run-in Period of study 3002, see
L538: Section 16.2.1. The planned trial duration is 30 months. The study schematic is shown in Figure 10. Refer to Section 16.2 for more details on the study protocol.
L539: Figure 10. Study Design, Study 3002
L540: Source: Study 3002 Protocol, p. 38.
L541: Abbreviations: CSL312, garadacimab; EOT, end of treatment; HAE, hereditary angioedema; OL, open-label
L542: ## **Key Inclusion Criteria**
L543: - Successful completion of trial 2001 or 3001.
OR
- For garadacimab-naïve subjects to enter the Run-in Period of the trial, they must meet the following criteria:Age \ge12 years.
- Diagnosed with C1-INH as defined by documented clinical history consistent with HAE, C1-INH antigen concentration and/or functional activity \le50% of normal, and C4 antigen concentration below the lower limit of the reference range as documented in the subject's medical record.
- Documentation of \ge3 HAE attacks during the 3 months before screening. For subjects taking HAE prophylaxis, the \ge3 HAE attacks could be documented over 3 consecutive months before commencing the prophylactic therapy.
- For garadacimab-naïve subjects to exit the Run-in Period and enter the Treatment Period, they must meet the following criteria:Participated in the Run-in Period for at least 1 month.
- Experienced at least an average of one HAE attack per month during the Run-in Period.
- No laboratory clinical abnormalities in the hematology, chemistry, or urinalysis assessments performed during screening.
- Verified C1-INH functional activity and antigen and C4 antigen concentration at screening.
L544: ## **Key Exclusion Criteria (for Garadacimab-Naïve Subjects)**
L545: - Concomitant diagnosis of another form of angioedema, such as idiopathic or acquired or recurrent angioedema associated with urticaria.
- Use of C1-INH products, androgens, antifibrinolytics or other small-molecule medications for routine prophylaxis against HAE attacks \ge2 weeks before the first day of the Run-in Period.
- Use of monoclonal antibodies (including lanadelumab) 3 months before the first day of the Run-in Period.
- Female subject's use of estrogen-containing medications with systemic absorption (e.g., oral contraceptive or hormonal replacement therapy) within 4 weeks prior to the Run-in Period.
L546: ## **7.6.2.2. Subject Disposition, Study 3002**
L547: Subject disposition as of the cutoff date of February 13, 2023, is summarized in Table 27. As the study is ongoing, the majority of subjects, 146 (90%), are continuing to receive garadacimab treatment. A total of 79 garadacimab-naïve subjects consented to participate in study 3002, two subjects discontinued the study prior to the Run-in Period and eight subjects discontinued during the Run-in Period. The most common reason for discontinuation during the Run-in Period was an insufficient number of HAE attacks (five subjects).

L583: ## 7.6.2.8. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Study 3002
L584: The review analyzed the AE dataset for TEAEs occurring in \ge 5\% of the total subjects by PT, as shown in Table 32. Similar to the results from trial 3001, nasopharyngitis/upper respiratory infection was one of the most common TEAEs. COVID-19 infection, however, was the most common TEAE. The high frequency of COVID-19 events is not unexpected given the timing of study initiation on March 29, 2021, during the midst of the COVID-19 pandemic, and these events are unlikely to be related to garadacimab. The majority of TEAEs were mild or moderate in severity for both rollover and garadacimab-naïve subjects (Table 29), which is reflective of trial 3001. The most common mild or moderate TEAEs were nasopharyngitis and COVID-19. The reported severe TEAEs were diarrhea, tendonitis, nasopharyngitis, toothache, headache, pulpitis, gastroenteritis, mood swings, and COVID-19. The number of subjects who reported each TEAE was small, one or two subjects each. All the severe TEAEs resolved without changing the dose or stopping garadacimab treatment (except for mood swing, as described in Table 31).

L603: ## 7.6.2.9. Laboratory Findings and Vital Signs, Study 3002
L604: Routine clinical testing, including hematology, serum chemistry with liver function tests, and coagulation studies, was performed every 3 months. Although study 3002 lacked a comparator group, lab and vital sign findings while on treatment did not detect any new safety signals. A discussion of the abnormal coagulation laboratory values can be found under Review Issues in Section 7.7.1.
L605: Figure 11 shows a screening assessment for potential cases of drug-induced liver injury. There were no potential Hy's law cases. Two subjects reported a maximum AST or ALT &gt;5\times ULN; however, both subjects had elevated AST and/or ALT at screening and confounding factors such as prior danazol use and past medical history of alcohol use and hepatic steatosis. The AST and ALT levels for these two subjects fluctuated over time with no apparent correlation to garadacimab administration.
L606: Figure 11. Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening Plot, Safety Population, Study 3002
L607: Source: adlb.xpt; software: R.
Each data point represents a patient plotted by their maximum ALT or AST versus their maximum total bilirubin values in the postbaseline period.
L608: A potential Hy's Law case (red circle) was defined as having any postbaseline total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2\times ULN within 30 days after a postbaseline ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3\times ULN, and ALP less than 2\times ULN (note ALP values are not circled). All patients with at least one postbaseline ALT or AST and bilirubin are plotted. The within-30-days analysis window rule does not apply to cholestatic DILI and temple's corollary cases.

L611: ## 7.6.3. Safety Results, Trial 2001
L612: Treatment Period 1 of trial 2001 was a phase 2, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel-group, dose-ranging trial in 44 adults with HAE. The trial randomized 32 subjects to one of three dosage regimens (75 mg Q4 weeks, 200 mg Q4 weeks, 600 mg Q4 weeks) and assigned an additional six subjects to open-label garadacimab 400 mg Q2 weeks and an additional six subjects with Type III HAE to open-label garadacimab 600 mg Q4 weeks. A total of eight subjects received the proposed dose of garadacimab 200 mg Q4 weeks. See Section 15.3.1 for details of the study design. Overall, the safety results from Treatment Period 1 of trial 2001 were consistent with those of trial 3001; no new risks were identified.
L613: ## 7.6.4. 120-Day Safety Update
L614: The Applicant submitted a 120-day safety update for this BLA with a data cutoff of June 13, 2023, that consisted solely of data from study 3002, the only ongoing clinical investigation. Updated safety data from study 3002 included no deaths, one drug rash SAE due to dipyrrone, and one treatment discontinuation due to an ISR. The ISR consisted of a large itchy wheal, starting within 24 h of garadacimab administration, that was mild in severity and resolved after 5 days. Commonly reported TEAEs were generally consistent with the safety profile observed to date. The safety update did not identify any new or concerning safety signals for garadacimab.
L615: ## 7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
L616: ## 7.7.1. Abnormal Coagulation Labs and Risk of Bleeding or Thromboembolism
L617: ## Issue
L618: Because of its mechanism of action, garadacimab has the potential to cause prolongation of coagulation parameters and result in bleeding or thromboembolic AEs.
L619: ## Background
L620: Garadacimab is a monoclonal antibody that inhibits the catalytic subunit of FXIIa. FXII is the first component of the intrinsic coagulation pathway and is typically activated on contact with substances that reflect tissue injury or damage, such as nucleic acids, heparin, collagen, and polyphosphates, to promote coagulation. Although inhibition of FXIIa might theoretically cause abnormal coagulation and a predisposition to bleeding, patients with genetic FXII deficiency demonstrate prolonged aPTT, but rarely exhibit a bleeding tendency. On the contrary, some literature suggests that FXII deficiency is associated with an increased risk of thrombosis; however, the data are inconclusive.
L621: ## Assessment
L622: Prolongations of aPTT were observed in nonclinical studies conducted in monkeys and rabbits, as well as in human subjects in the phase 1 and 2 clinical trials. In trial 3001 and OLE study 3002, the Applicant prospectively monitored coagulation parameters and bleeding or thromboembolic events as AESIs. Subjects with clinically significant bleeding due to coagulopathy or thrombotic disorders were excluded from enrollment, but subjects were allowed to receive anticoagulants (i.e., Factor X inhibitor), antiplatelet medications, or tranexamic acid as concomitant medications during the trials.
L623: ## Activated Partial Thromboplastin Time
L624: The aPTT was obtained every 3 months during trial 3001 and study 3002. In trial 3001, 3 of 39 subjects in the garadacimab treatment group had aPTT prolongation (normal range 24.3 to 43.4), as shown in Table 35. Two of the three subjects had a solitary occurrence of aPTT elevation that self-resolved without garadacimab dose modification or treatment discontinuation. None of the subjects experienced clinical manifestations of coagulation pathway abnormalities. Subject (b) (6) reported taking apixaban (Factor X inhibitor) for a history of bilateral pulmonary embolism. Otherwise, aPTT prolongations did not correspond to concomitant medication use.

L629: Further analyses were conducted exploring the real-time relationship between garadacimab plasma concentrations and aPTT prolongation using data from subjects with HAE in trial 3001, which evaluated the proposed maintenance dose of 200 mg SC once monthly. The relationship between garadacimab plasma concentration and aPTT was generally flat in trial 3001 when subjects received the proposed maintenance dose of 200 mg SC once monthly (see Figure 12). Some data points fell above the general trend, and these represent subjects with prolonged aPTT. These subjects have been described above.

L632: ## Prothrombin Time
L633: As presented in Table 36, there were a total of six subjects with prolonged prothrombin time (PT) &gt;1.3 \times ULN (normal range 9.8 to 12.1) in the garadacimab group (two of whom are mentioned above) and one subject in the placebo group (not shown). Most prolongations were singular events that resolved without garadacimab dose modification or treatment discontinuation. There was no clear correlation with other concomitant medications or other underlying conditions or risk factors. None of the subjects experienced clinical manifestations of bleeding diatheses or thromboemboli. Again, of note, subject (b) (6) reported taking apixaban (Factor X inhibitor), which may prolong PT.

L638: The international normalized ratio (INR) is a calculation of the subject's PT divided by the control PT. INR = \frac{\text{Subject Prothrombin Time}}{\text{Control Prothrombin Time}}. The review team also independently reviewed the INR values of the garadacimab and placebo groups in trial 3001. The same six subjects in Table 36 also had INR &gt; 1.3 \times ULN with none exceeding an INR &gt; 2.2 \times ULN.
L639: ## Pooled PK Analysis by PT
L640: The real-time relationship between garadacimab plasma concentrations and PT was explored. No apparent E-R relationship between plasma trough concentrations and PT was found (see Figure 13). Some data points fell above the general trend, and these represent subjects with prolonged PT. These subjects have been described above.

L645: ## Bleeding and Thromboembolism Adverse Events
L646: The review team analyzed AE datasets for TEAEs related to bleeding or thromboembolism by PTs, SMQs (hemorrhage, embolic and thrombotic events), and FMQs (hemorrhage, thrombosis). Across the garadacimab clinical development program, there were no deaths, serious or severe bleeding/thromboembolic AEs, or events leading to treatment discontinuation. In trial 3001, no
L647: thromboembolic events occurred. The broad and narrow FMQs for hemorrhage identified two TEAEs in two subjects in the garadacimab treatment group (Table 37). Both cases self-resolved and are more accurately characterized as injury-induced bruising rather than representative of an increased risk for spontaneous hemorrhage or bleeding.
L648: Similarly, in study 3002, there were no thromboembolic events reported. The most commonly reported bleeding related TEAE in study 3002 was contusion (bruising), reported in four subjects. The other bleeding related TEAEs, each reported by one subject, consisted of epistaxis, injection site bruising, injection site hematoma, intermenstrual bleeding, and vaginal bleeding. The female subjects who reported intermenstrual bleeding and vaginal bleeding were both &lt;50 years of age. All bleeding related TEAEs were rated as mild in severity, associated with normal coagulation laboratory values, and did not result in treatment discontinuation.
L649: In trial 2001, one subject who received garadacimab 600 mg Q4 weeks (with a 300 mg IV loading dose) experienced mild spontaneous epistaxis that self-resolved the same day. Treatment with garadacimab continued at the same dose with no further bleeding. There was a single TEAE of vessel puncture site thrombosis in the 200 mg Q4 week group that is unlikely related to study drug.
L650: Lastly, the review team also examined concomitant use of anticoagulants, antiplatelet or blood-thinning medications and identified four subjects with concomitant medication use. One subject (ID [REDACTED](b)(6)), described above, was taking apixaban. Two subjects were on tranexamic acid (antiplasmin) and one subject was on ticagrelor (antiplatelet). None of the four subjects reported a bleeding event, and only the subject taking apixaban had abnormal coagulation laboratory markers.

L656: ## **Conclusion**
L657: The prolongations in aPTT and PT coagulation parameters associated with garadacimab treatment do not exhibit an E-R relationship and appear to be asymptomatic without an increased risk for bleeding or thromboembolic adverse reactions. No safety signal emerged for bleeding or thromboemboli irrespective of coagulation markers. Prolongation of coagulation markers without an increased bleeding risk has also been observed with other approved HAE therapies that target the intrinsic coagulation pathway. Therefore, this finding might be related to the mechanism of action of garadacimab and/or interaction between the drug and laboratory test. Information regarding asymptomatic aPTT/PT prolongation may be useful for healthcare providers and will be added to the draft PI.
L658: ## 7.7.2. Hypersensitivity Reactions
L659: ## **Issue**
L660: Does the garadacimab safety data warrant a Warnings and Precautions statement for hypersensitivity reactions?
L661: ## **Background**
L662: Hypersensitivity reactions are frequently associated with injectable products and the therapeutic class of monoclonal antibodies, regardless of the therapeutic target. As such, hypersensitivity reactions were prespecified as an AESI in trial 3001 and study 3002.
L663: The Warnings and Precautions section is intended to include serious or clinically significant adverse reactions in which there is reasonable evidence of causal relationship between the drug and the AE. Considerations for evidence of causal relationship include frequency of reporting and whether the rate of the event was greater in drug compared to placebo.
L664: ## **Assessment**
L665: The review team analyzed the TEAEs in trial 3001 and study 3002 by PT, narrow and broad FMQ for hypersensitivity, and SMQ for hypersensitivity. No hypersensitivity TEAEs were reported with garadacimab treatment in trial 3001. In study 3002, only the FMQ (Broad) and the SMQ searches yielded TEAEs related to hypersensitivity; however, none of the PTs were specific or clinically relevant to an immediate hypersensitivity reaction (i.e., allergic rhinitis, eczema, pruritus).
L666: ## **Conclusion**
L667: Immediate hypersensitivity reactions would not be an unexpected safety signal; however, no events occurred in trial 3001 or study 3002. Per labeling policy, Warnings and Precautions are reserved for observed, rather than theoretical, risks except in the case of drug class labeling. Hypersensitivity is not considered class labeling for monoclonal antibodies.
L668: ## 7.7.3. Injection Site Reactions
L669: ## Issue
L670: ISRs are a common adverse reaction from SC injectable drug products and were observed during the garadacimab development program. The Applicant proposes to include ISRs (b) (4) in Section 6 of the PI.
L671: ## Background
L672: In the phase 2, dose-ranging trial 2001 (see Section 15.3), there was a dose-dependent increase in ISRs. ISRs occurred in one (11.1%), one (12.5%), and four (57.1%) subjects in the 75 mg, 200 mg, and 600 mg dose groups, respectively. Because of the relatively high proportion of ISRs in the 600 mg dose group, the Applicant carried forward the 200 mg dose for evaluation in phase 3.
L673: ## Assessment
L674: Analyses of AE datasets for trial 3001 and study 3002 by Local Administration Reaction FMQ (Narrow and Broad) are shown in Table 38 and Table 39. The narrow and broad search queries produced the same results. In trial 3001, there was no significant difference in ISRs between the garadacimab and placebo treatment groups during the placebo-controlled 6-month Treatment Period.
L675: ISRs were reported by both treatment-naïve and rollover subjects in study 3002, with a higher frequency in the rollover subjects. Most of the ISRs for the garadacimab-naïve subjects were reported within the first 6 months of starting garadacimab. One subject in the garadacimab-naïve treatment group discontinued treatment due to ISR (Section 7.6.2.7).

L684: ## Conclusion
L685: Local ISRs occurred more frequently with placebo in trial 3001, and therefore will not be listed as an adverse drug reaction from this trial. However, given the higher frequency of ISRs reported in study 3002 and the high likelihood that these events are treatment-related adverse drug reactions, information regarding the ISRs observed in study 3002 will be included in the draft PI.
L686: ## 8. Therapeutic Individualization
L687: ## 8.1. Intrinsic Factors
L688: Based on population PK analyses, intrinsic factors including age (12 to 73 years), [REDACTED], and race were not found to meaningfully influence the PK of garadacimab after body weight was introduced as a covariate. No dedicated studies have been conducted to evaluate the impact of renal impairment or hepatic impairment on the PK of garadacimab. Per the population PK analysis, renal impairment categories (estimated glomerular filtration rate \ge 90 mL/min/1.73 m2 [normal, N=145]; 60 to &lt;90 mL/min/1.73 m2 [mild, N=26]; and 30 to &lt;60 mL/min/1.73 m2 [moderate, N=2]) did not impact the PK of garadacimab. In addition, bilirubin (4 to 13 \mumol/L; 10th and 90th percentiles, respectively, in the observed dataset) did not have a large impact on garadacimab clearance.
L689: For additional details on population PK analyses, refer to Section 14.5.
L690: ## 8.1.1. Body Weight
L691: Overall, no dosage adjustments are recommended based on body weight.
L692: In population PK analysis, body weight was identified as a significant covariate impacting the clearance and volume of distribution of garadacimab. In general, garadacimab exposure is higher in subjects with lower body weight. The impact of various covariates, including body weight, on garadacimab AUCtau,ss is shown in Figure 14. The median AUCtau,ss in a subject weighing 105 kg or weighing 55 kg is predicted to be about 38% lower or 32% higher, respectively, relative to the reference AUCtau,ss of a typical patient weighing 70 kg. Although differences in exposure are predicted based on body weight, no clear trend in monthly HAE attack rate by baseline weight quartile was observed in trial 3001 (Figure 15).
L693: These observations are supported by results from trial 2001, which evaluated various garadacimab dosing regimens ranging from 75 mg every Q4W to 600 mg SC Q4W over a 13-week double-blind Treatment Period. A reduction relative to placebo in the mean time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month was observed for all dosages evaluated with no apparent dose-response relationship observed from 75 mg to 600 mg. As the observed monthly HAE attack rate in the 200 mg group was numerically the lowest across all treatment groups, the 200-mg dose was carried over to the phase 3 trial 3001. For additional details, refer to Section 14.2.4.
L694: **Figure 14. Univariate Forest Plot Illustrating Covariate Effects on Garadacimab Steady-State AUC_{\tau} Relative to a Reference Subject**
L695: Source: Figure S82, Population PK/PD Report.
L696: Covariates in the model were visualized by varying single covariates individually while keeping all other conditions constant. Results are presented relative to a reference subject, i.e., a non-Japanese, non-Chinese, healthy volunteer with baseline body weight of 70 kg, baseline serum creatinine of 0.75 mg/dL, baseline ALT of 25 U/L, and baseline bilirubin of 8\mumol/L. Colored diamonds represent the median and the solid horizontal lines represent the 95% confidence interval. The gray shaded reference range of 80 to 125% was analogous to the region of practical equivalence. For continuous covariates, the upper and lower values used in the simulations were determined based on the 10th and 90th percentiles in the observed dataset.
L697: Abbreviations: AUC_{\tau,ss}, area under the plasma concentration-time curve over one dosing interval at steady state; BLALT, baseline alanine aminotransferase; BLBILI, baseline bilirubin; BLCREAT, baseline serum creatinine BLWT, baseline body weight; CI, confidence interval; HAE, hereditary angioedema; PD, pharmacodynamics; PK, pharmacokinetics
L698: Figure 15. Monthly HAE Attack Rate Over Time by Baseline Weight Quartile, Trial 3001
L699: Source: Figure S31, Exposure-Response Report.
Solid circles represent the mean monthly HAE attack rate. Vertical bars represent the 95% confidence interval of the mean monthly HAE attack rate. Colors represent different covariate levels.
Abbreviation: HAE, hereditary angioedema; Q, quartile
L700: The proposed population for garadacimab includes adolescent subjects with HAE aged 12 years and older. Adolescent subjects with HAE were enrolled in trial 3001 (N=6) and study 3002 (N=10, including 5 rollover subjects from trial 3001). In both studies, the median body weight among adolescents receiving garadacimab was lower than the overall median body weight among all subjects receiving garadacimab (trial 3001: 60.3 kg and 78.0 kg for adolescents and overall, respectively; study 3002: 55.7 kg and 78.3 kg for adolescents and overall, respectively).
L701: Despite the lower body weight in adolescents relative to the overall population enrolled in trial 3001, there was no apparent difference in the observed garadacimab trough concentrations throughout the Treatment Period (Figure 16). In study 3002, garadacimab trough concentrations were 1.2 to 1.8-fold greater in adolescents relative to adults from Month 3 through Month 12 (Figure 17). Note that only three adolescent subjects provided data at Month 12, the time point that showed 1.8-fold greater exposure among adolescents. Data at Month 12 should be interpreted with caution.
L702: **Figure 16. Mean (SD) Garadacimab Trough Concentration-Time Profiles in Adults and Adolescents, Trial 3001**
L703: Source: Figure 14.2.5.2.3, Clinical Study Report for trial 3001.
Abbreviations: CSL312, garadacimab; SD, standard deviation
L704: **Figure 17. Mean (SD) Garadacimab Trough Concentration-Time Profiles in Adults and Adolescents, Study 3002**
L705: Source: Figure 14.2.5.2.3, Clinical Study Report for study 3002.
Abbreviations: CSL312, garadacimab; SD, standard deviation
L706: In study 3002, peak plasma concentrations at steady state in adolescents were achieved at 3 to 7 days postdose, with mean (SD) concentrations of 21.9 (15.4) \mug/mL on Day 3 and 21.7 (11.5) \mug/mL on Day 7. These values are approximately 1.4-fold greater than the observed steady state C_{max} following 200 mg SC Q4W from adults in trial 2001 (mean [SD] C_{max}=15.9 [5.2] \mug/mL), and 2.6-fold lower than the observed steady state C_{max} following 600 mg SC Q4W from adults in trial 2001 (mean [SD] C_{max}=56.4 [15.9] \mug/mL).
L707: Population PK simulations following the proposed dosage (i.e., 400 mg SC loading dose, then 200 mg SC Q4W) in adults and adolescents predict 1.33-fold greater C_{max} at steady state and 1.36-fold greater AUC_{tau,ss} in adolescents relative to adults (mean [SD] adolescent [N=10,000] C_{max} at steady state and AUC_{tau,ss}=27.3 [14.4] \mug/mL and 13,700 [6670] \mug\cdoth/mL, respectively; mean [SD] adult [N=10,000] C_{max} at steady state and AUC_{tau,ss}=20.5 [10.0] \mug/mL and 10100 [4400] \mug\cdoth/mL, respectively) (data derived from Table SYN6, Population PK/Pharmacodynamic Report).
L708: For additional details on garadacimab PK in adolescents in trial 3001 and study 3002, refer to Sections 14.2.5 and 14.2.6. For additional details on population PK, refer to Section 14.5.
L709: ## 8.1.2. Type III (FXII/PLG mutation) HAE
L710: Refer to Section 6.3.2.
L711: ## 8.2. Extrinsic Factors
L712: Dedicated drug-drug interaction studies with garadacimab have not been conducted. As garadacimab is a monoclonal antibody, direct drug-drug interactions via inhibition or induction of cytochrome P450 enzymes or transporters are not expected. The Applicant conducted a population PK analysis to evaluate whether use of concomitant medications had an impact on garadacimab steady-state exposure. The AUC_{tau,ss} of garadacimab was similar regardless of whether subjects used analgesics, anti-inflammatory medications, antihistamines, or antibacterials (Figure 18). Simulated garadacimab exposure was also evaluated depending on whether subjects used rescue medications for treatment of HAE attacks. Similar garadacimab AUC_{tau,ss} was observed regardless of whether complement C1-INH or icatibant acetate was used (Figure 19). Note that Figure 19 also includes box plots for conestat alfa and a category of “Other Rescue Medicine.” No subjects used conestat alfa while two subjects were categorized as using other rescue medications. Thus, their impact on steady state garadacimab exposure could not be evaluated.

L718: ## 8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
L719: Garadacimab was granted Orphan Drug Designation in April 2020 for bradykinin-mediated angioedema (which includes hereditary angioedema) and is therefore exempt from Pediatric Research Equity Act study requirements.
L720: (b) (4)
L721: ## 8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males of Reproductive Potential
L722: The potential reproductive toxicity of garadacimab was evaluated in fertility and early embryonic development studies with male and female rabbits, an embryofetal development study with female rabbits, and a pre- and postnatal development study with female rabbits. Rabbits were determined to be a pharmacologically relevant species.
L723: ## Fertility and Developmental Toxicology
L724: In the male fertility and early embryonic development study, male rabbits were treated with garadacimab (IV doses of 10, 30, or 100 mg/kg q3 days) for 4 weeks prior to mating, during the mating period, and continuing to Postpairing Day 8. Females were not dosed and assigned to each group for mating purposes only. There were no treatment-related effects on mating and
L725: fertility parameters, reproductive indices, or sperm assessments. The no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) for this study was the HD of 100 mg/kg/dose based on a lack of findings.
L726: In the female fertility and early embryonic development study, female rabbits were treated with garadacimab (IV doses of 10, 30, or 100 mg/kg q3 days) for 2 weeks prior to mating, during the mating period, and continuing to Gestation Day 8. Males were not dosed and were assigned to each group for mating purposes only. No garadacimab-related effects on maternal reproductive parameters or embryo survival were observed. The NOAEL for female fertility and embryo survival was the HD, 100 mg/kg/dose.
L727: ## Embryo-Fetal Development
L728: In the embryo-fetal development study, pregnant rabbits were administered IV doses of 10, 30, and 100 mg/kg q3 days on Gestation Days 6, 9, 12, 15 and 18. Necropsies were conducted on Gestation Day 29. There was no effect on treated females observed related to garadacimab treatment. There were no treatment-related external, visceral, or skeletal major or minor abnormalities. The NOAEL for maternal and fetal toxicity was concluded to be 100 mg/kg/occasion (HD).
L729: ## Prenatal and Postnatal Development
L730: In the rabbit pre-and postnatal development study, pregnant rabbits (F0) were treated with garadacimab from Gestation Day 7 through Lactation Day 38. SC doses of 0, 10 (low dose), 30 (mid dose), and 100 (HD) mg/kg/dose and an IV dose of 100 (HD) mg/kg were administered once every 5 days on Gestation Day 7, 12, 17, 22, and 27 and Lactation Days 3, 8, 13, 18, 23, 28, 33, and 38. The F1 generation was not directly administered the test article. Prolongation of aPTT values and toxicokinetic indicated that drug exposure was continuous during the gestation and lactation periods in F0 maternal animals.
L731: On Gestation Day 29, fetal parturition plasma garadacimab concentrations were 76.6%, 136.6%, and 40.8% of the maternal concentrations, at SC low dose, SC mid dose, and SC HD, respectively. At IV HD, maternal and fetal concentrations were approximately equivalent. Garadacimab was able to cross the placenta.
L732: For F0 dams, no garadacimab-related effects were observed on mortality, clinical signs, body weight, natural delivery, litter data, or maternal macroscopic observations. The maternal NOAEL was 100 mg/kg/dose (HD) SC/IV.
L733: For the F1 generation, no garadacimab-related effects were observed on mortality, clinical signs, body weight, developmental signs, functional observation battery, eye-blink response, presence of motile sperm, reproductive performance, cesarean section parameters, or preweaning or postweaning macroscopic observations. In the F1 generation, the NOAEL for fetal development was 100 mg/kg/dose (HD) SC/IV. The NOAEL for developmental, neurobehavioral, and reproductive performance in the F1 generation was 100 mg/kg/dose (HD) SC/IV.
L734: Prolongation of aPTT was observed in all four studies with F0 male and female rabbits, but not associated with any bleeding events. aPTT was not measured in F1 rabbits in the pre-and postnatal development study.
L735: ## 9. Product Quality
L736: ## Complete Response With Quality Issues
L737: Although the Office of Pharmaceutical Quality review team has assessed BLA 761367 with respect to drug substance and immunogenicity assay and has determined that it meets all of those applicable standards to support the identity, strength, quality, and purity that it purports, the facility, microbiology and DP disciplines have unresolved quality issues. The DP facility (CSL Behring GmbH, Marburg, Germany, FEI 3003098680) recommendation is Withhold. The DP process design is inadequate to minimize potential contamination of the sterile DP, and the DP process control and process validation data are insufficient to ensure product quality consistency and process performance consistency. Therefore, from an Office of Pharmaceutical Quality perspective, this BLA is not deemed ready for approval in its present form until the above-mentioned issues are satisfactorily resolved. As such, Office of Pharmaceutical Quality recommends a complete response action from facility, microbiology, and product quality perspectives.
L738: ## 9.1. Device or Combination Product Considerations
L739: The Applicant proposed two to-be-marketed DP presentations for SC administration: 1) a 200 mg/1.2 mL prefilled syringe with needle safety device (PFS-NSD) and a 200 mg/1.2 mL autoinjector (AI). The Division of Drug Delivery, General Hospital &amp; Human Factors in the Center for Devices and Radiological Health reviewed the device descriptions, labeling, design controls, risk analysis, and design verification of the AI and PFS-NSD devices, and determined that both the PFS-NSD and the AI devices are approvable. The essential performance requirements including design specifications, verification method, validation, biocompatibility and stability were found to be acceptable for both the PFS-NSD and the AI (for full details see the Center for Devices and Radiological Health review submitted on May 28, 2024 and August 21, 2024).
L740: In the pivotal trial 3001, only the PFS-NSD presentation was used; however, the AI presentation was administered in the OLE study 3002. The Applicant conducted a phase 1, relative bioavailability bridging study 1004, to compare the PK of garadacimab following a single SC injection as the PFS-NSD or the AI; the results are presented in Section 14.2.
L741: ## 10. Human Subjects Protections/Clinical Site and Other Good Clinical Practice Inspections/Financial Disclosure Review
L742: ## Human Subjects Protections
L743: As stated by the Applicant, the clinical trials were conducted in compliance with Good Clinical Practice, as required by the ICH guidance for industry *E6 Good Clinical Practice* (November 2016). The clinical studies also met the requirements of the Declaration of Helsinki and, where
L744: applicable, national laws and regulations regarding ethical committee review, informed consent, and protection of human subjects participating in biomedical research. All studies submitted in this application were conducted in accordance with FDA requirements under IND 139936.
L745: ## **Clinical Site Inspections**
L746: FDA clinical site inspections were performed at two sites in the United States for trial 3001 and study 3002. Refer to Section 22 for details.
L747: ## **Financial Disclosure**
L748: The Applicant adequately disclosed financial interests/arrangements with clinical investigators as recommended in the guidance for industry, Financial Disclosure by Clinical Investigators (February 2013) and by 21 Code of Federal Regulations 54.4. None of the investigators in the clinical studies were employed by the Applicant. Three clinical investigators disclosed financial interests and arrangements with the Applicant (see Section 25). To minimize bias, an independent data monitoring committee was established to monitor the safety and efficacy data generated from the clinical trials. Additionally, the small number of subjects who participated at each site also minimized the potential for bias.
L749: In conclusion, the likelihood that trial results were biased on financial interests is minimal and should not affect the approvability of the application.
L750: ## **11. Advisory Committee Summary**
L751: An advisory committee was not held for this application. The application did not raise challenging efficacy or safety issues that required external input.

L756: ## 13. Pharmacology Toxicology
L757: ## 13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted With the Investigational New Drug Application
L758: ## 13.1.1. Pharmacology (Primary and Secondary)
L759: The nonclinical pharmacology studies were originally reviewed under the IND. Summaries are provided here.
L760: ## Primary Pharmacology
L761: ## **In vitro**
L762: Garadacimab is a fully humanized, affinity matured monoclonal antibody that was developed to bind specifically to activated FXII (FXIIa) and render it inactivated, thereby arresting kallikrein-kinin system activation and contact-mediated thrombosis without affecting hemostasis. Using an amino acid mutagenesis approach, it was shown that garadacimab binds to the catalytic site of FXIIa.
L763: Effects of garadacimab on Factor XII (FXII) activity were evaluated in human, cynomolgus monkey, mouse and rabbit plasma blood samples (Table 40, Table 41, Table 42, and Table 43). Inhibition of FXII activity was similar in human and monkey. In contrast, inhibition of FXII activity was lower in mouse and rabbit. Inhibition of FXII with garadacimab was observed in all nonhuman species tested. The affinity matured aFXII antagonist, garadacimab, has high affinity to human \betaFXIIa (140pM) and is cross-reactive with mouse, rabbit, and cynomolgus \betaFXIIa (700pM, 400pM and 19nM, respectively).

Abbreviations: k_a, absorption rate constant; k_d, dissociation constant; K_D, equilibrium dissociation constant
L777: Using dextran sulfate activated plasma, garadacimab inhibited FXII activity, resulting in lowered production of bradykinin.
L778: Garadacimab was shown to only antagonize FXIIa enzymatic activities, displaying 100% inhibition of FXIIa catalytic activity for both the \alpha and \beta fragments; this means that zymogen FXII functions were negligibly impacted. In an in vitro chromogenic assay, garadacimab inhibited the enzymatic activity of human plasma-derived FXIIa and \betaFXIIa at equivalent levels (half-maximal inhibitory concentration \sim15nM). Garadacimab produced a prolongation of activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) clotting times in standard human plasma.
L779: Garadacimab was evaluated for its ability to inhibit FXII activity in blood samples collected from subjects with hereditary angioedema (HAE). Plasma FXII concentrations and activities were analyzed in HAE Type I, II, III, and acquired angioedema samples. Elevated FXII activities were observed in HAE Type I and III samples compared to control samples. No differences in FXII activities were observed with HAE Type II or acquired angioedema samples. Garadacimab inhibited FXIIa activity following activation of plasma samples from normal, HAE Types I, II, III, and C1-esterase inhibitor (C1-INH)-AAE donors with dextran sulfate. Dextran sulfate activates plasma via initial contact activation of FXII and models initiation of an HAE attack, leading to bradykinin-mediated swelling. Garadacimab dose-dependently inhibited FXIIa chromogenic activity, with maximal FXIIa inhibition observed at 10 \mug/mL for all normal and patient plasma samples examined.
L780: ## **In Vivo**
L781: In an angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitor (captopril)-induced edema murine model (heterozygous C1-INH-deficient (C1INH^{+/-}) mice treated with 2.5 mg/kg captopril), garadacimab significantly inhibited the angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitor-induced increase of vascular permeability. Complete ablation of vascular leakage was observed at garadacimab doses of \ge0.5 mg/kg.
L782: In a murine passive cutaneous anaphylaxis model, FXII-dependent IgE-mediated activation of mast cells has been shown to increase vascular permeability by heparin-initiated bradykinin formation. In this model, garadacimab significantly inhibited FXIIa-mediated bradykinin formation.
L783: ## **Recommendation for Designation of the Established Pharmacological Classification**
L784: The recommended established pharmacologic class for garadacimab is “FXIIa inhibitor” (monoclonal antibody). Garadacimab is a fully humanized, affinity matured monoclonal antibody that was developed to bind specifically to FXIIa and renders it inactivated, thereby arresting kallikrein-kinin system activation and contact-mediated thrombosis without affecting hemostasis. Using an amino acid mutagenesis approach, it was shown that garadacimab binds to the catalytic site of FXIIa. It acts at a different point in coagulation cascade than the other drugs approved for the treatment of HAE.
L785: Ecallantide, lanadelumab, and Berotralstat are listed in the FDA established pharmacologic class Text Phrasing as plasma kallikrein inhibitors. These drugs bind to plasma kallikrein and inhibit its proteolytic activity. Plasma kallikrein is a protease that cleaves high-molecular-weight kininogen to generate cleaved high-molecular-weight kininogen and bradykinin.
L786: ## 13.1.2. Safety Pharmacology
L787: Safety pharmacology parameters were incorporated into the pivotal repeat-dose 26-week monkey toxicology study. With intravenous (IV) doses up to 100 mg/kg/week and subcutaneous (SC) doses up to 200 mg/kg/week, no treatment-related effects on electrocardiogram, blood pressure, respiration rate, or pulse rate were observed during the 26-week Treatment Period.
L788: In a mouse study evaluating respiratory parameters, garadacimab at IV doses of 3, 10, 30 and 100 mg/kg or SC doses of 60 and 200 mg/kg produced no statistically significant or biologically relevant effects on respiratory rate, tidal volume, or minute volume at any postdose time point. The no observed adverse effect levels (NOAELs) were identified as 100 mg/kg (IV) and 200 mg/kg (SC).
L789: ## 13.1.3. Absorption, Distribution, Metabolism, Excretion/Pharmacokinetics
L790: A single-dose pharmacokinetic (PK) study using the IV and SC routes was conducted in cynomolgus monkeys. The SC bioavailability of garadacimab (6 mg/kg) was ~66% in monkeys. Mean estimated t_{1/2} values for garadacimab in the single-dose PK study with monkeys ranged from 147 to 581 h (IV) and 206 to 424 h (SC).
L791: ## 13.1.4. Toxicology
L792: ## 13.1.4.1. General Toxicology
L793: ## Repeat-Dose Toxicity
L794: The Applicant characterized the toxicity profile of garadacimab by conducting 4-week mouse and 26-week monkey toxicology studies with garadacimab using the IV and SC routes. Immunogenicity in mice precluded conducting longer toxicology studies in this species.
L795: ## **CSL312: Repeat-Dose Toxicity Study by Intravenous and Subcutaneous Administration to CD-1 Mice for 4 Weeks Followed by an 8-Week Recovery Period (Study # [REDACTED](b) (4))**
L796: ## Key Study Findings
L797: - In a 4-week toxicity and toxicokinetic (TK) study, CD-1 mice (12/sex/group) received garadacimab at IV doses of 0, 3, 10, 30, and 100 mg/kg and SC doses of 0, 60, and 200 mg/kg (SC).
- There were 23 unscheduled deaths during the study (16 males and 7 females). The deaths of one HD IV male, one low dose (LD) SC male, and two LD SC females were considered to be due to the blood sampling procedure. The deaths and/or premature euthanasia of the remaining animals, primarily in the LD IV group, were attributed to immunogenicity based upon observed adverse clinical signs, although there were no histopathological findings.
L798: - Significant prolongations of the aPTT were observed for males (up to 127%) and females (up to 123%) in all garadacimab treatment groups (IV and SC) relative to control animals at the end of the 4-week Treatment Period.
- The target organs of toxicity were identified as the lymph nodes (axillary and inguinal) and SC injection sites. An increase in incidence and severity of minimal to slight inflammatory cell infiltrates at the SC injection sites (dorsal 1 and 2) were observed for females in the 60 and 200 mg/kg SC treatment groups at the end of the Treatment Period.
- An increased incidence and/or severity of minimal to slight lymphocyte cellularity in the axillary and/or inguinal lymph nodes was observed for males and/or females in the 60 and 200 mg/kg SC treatment groups at the end of the 4-week Treatment Period.
- Systemic exposure (maximum plasma concentration (C_{max})) and area under the concentration-time curve (AUC) from 0 to 96 h) to garadacimab increased in a dose-proportional manner for males and females from 3 to 100 mg/kg (Day 1) and 10 to 100 mg/kg (Day 29) following IV administration. Moderate accumulation of garadacimab was observed in the IV dosing groups (1.56 to 3.08) and SC groups (1.38 to 2.33) during the study period. No clear differences in systemic exposure between males and females were observed.
- The determined NOAEL was the high SC dose of 200 mg/kg.

L810: Parameter	Major Findings
Mortality	
There were 23 unscheduled deaths during the study (16 males and 7 females). The Applicant's report stated that four of the deaths (i.e., 100 mg/kg IV male, 60 mg/kg SC male, and two 60 mg/kg SC females) were due to the blood sampling procedure and not test article related.
Five males in the 3 mg/kg/day IV group and one female in the 30 mg/kg/day IV group were found dead during or immediately following the dose administration. An additional four animals were found dead on dosing days (3 mg/kg IV female, three 10 mg/kg IV females). A further nine animals were prematurely euthanized for welfare reasons, because of clinical signs observed (i.e., eight 3 mg/kg IV males and one 10 mg/kg IV male). Clinical signs prior to termination consisted of mainly swollen limbs and muzzle and/or reddening of the limbs or muzzle sometimes associated with signs of decreased activity, shallow or slow breathing and flattened posture. These deaths were most likely due to immunogenicity based on adverse clinical signs that occurred shortly after dosing. The remaining 14 low dose males and 29 low dose females were terminated on Day 18 following the sixth dose and Days 12-13 after the fourth dose, respectively. Terminal procedures were conducted on low dose males and females after sacrifice.

Clinical chemistry	
Significant increases in potassium levels were observed for males (up to 18%) and females (up to 17%) at ≥30 mg/kg/day garadacimab (IV) relative to control animals. A significant increase in potassium levels were also observed for males (up to 13%) in both SC treatment groups. Clear dose-dependent effects were not observed. These findings are clinically monitorable.
Slight increases in total protein levels were observed for males (up to 4%) in all IV treatment groups relative to control animals. Clear dose-dependent effects were not observed. These findings were considered clinically monitorable.
Significant increases in A1 and gamma globulin protein fractions were observed for males (up to 100%) in all treatment groups (A1) and at ≥30 mg/kg/day garadacimab IV (gamma) relative to control animals.
Significant increases in gamma globulin protein fractions were also observed for males (up to 100%) and females (up to 100%) in SC treatment groups relative to control animals. Clear dose-dependent effects were not observed. These findings are clinically monitorable.
Significant decreases in phosphorous levels were observed for females (up to 20%) in the SC treatment groups relative to control animals. These findings are clinically monitorable.

Gross pathology	
An increased incidence of enlarged axillary and/or inguinal lymph nodes were observed in males and females at ≥60 mg/kg/day garadacimab (SC) relative to control animals. These findings correlated with histopathological findings of minimal to slight increased generalized cellularity in the inguinal and axillary lymph nodes in the SC treatment groups.
L817: Parameter	Major Findings
Organ weights	Liver weights were slightly decreased for males in the 3-10 mg/kg IV treatment groups and for males in the 200 mg/kg SC treatment group. Liver weights were also slightly decreased for females at \ge 30 mg/kg IV and 200 mg/kg SC treatment groups. These findings were reversible. There were no correlating histopathological findings at the end of the Treatment Period.
Histopathology
Adequate battery: Yes	
The potential target organs of toxicity were identified as the lymph nodes (axillary and inguinal) and subcutaneous injection sites.
An increase in incidence and severity of minimal to slight inflammatory cell infiltrates at the subcutaneous injection sites (dorsal 1 and 2) were observed for females in the 60 and 200 mg/kg SC treatment groups at the end of the Treatment Period. The Applicant's report stated that this finding was characterized mainly by a diffuse neutrophilic infiltrate in the subcutaneous connective tissue. Minimal inflammatory cell infiltrates at the subcutaneous injection sites (dorsal 1 and 2) were also observed for males (2/12) in the 200 mg/kg SC treatment group. These findings were reversible. These findings were not considered dose-limiting and are clinically monitorable.
An increase in incidence and/or severity of minimal to slight lymphocyte cellularity in the axillary and/or inguinal lymph nodes was observed for males and/or females in the 60 and 200 mg/kg SC treatment groups at the end of the 4-week Treatment Period. These findings were reversible and were not considered adverse. These findings correlated with macroscopic findings of enlarged lymph nodes (axillary and inguinal) for selected males and females in these treatment groups. The Applicant's report stated that these findings were considered a localized, secondary response to the inflammatory infiltrate observed at the subcutaneous injection sites primarily in females treated with 200 mg/kg garadacimab (SC).

Toxicokinetics	Systemic exposure (C_{max} and AUC_{0-96}) to garadacimab increased in a dose-proportional manner for males and females from 3 to 100 mg/kg (Day 1) and 10 to 100 mg/kg (Day 29) following IV administration. Moderate accumulation of garadacimab was observed in the IV dosing groups (1.56-3.08) and SC groups (1.38-2.33) during the study period. No clear differences in systemic exposure between males and females were observed; however, systemic exposure (C_{max} and AUC_{0-96}) was slightly higher in females than males following SC administration of garadacimab. Toxicokinetic data are summarized in Table 47.
Immunogenicity:	According to the Applicant's report, four samples (i.e., three 10 mg/kg IV females and a 60 mg/kg SC female) were confirmed positive for ADAs against garadacimab.

L824: ## CSL312: Toxicity Study by Intravenous (Bolus) and Subcutaneous Administration to Cynomolgus Monkeys for 26 Weeks Followed by an 8-Week Recovery Period (Study # (b) (4))
L825: ## Key Study Findings
L826: - In a 26-week toxicity and TK study, cynomolgus monkeys (4 sex/group), received garadacimab at IV doses of 0, 10, 30, and 100 mg/kg/week and SC doses of 0, 60, and 200 mg/kg/week.
- There were two unscheduled deaths in the study. One HD SC female was euthanized on Day 66 due to an immunogenic reaction (i.e., adverse clinical signs and detection of antidrug
L827: antibodies (ADAs)). One HD IV male was euthanized on Day 119 due to rapid onset of adverse clinical signs consistent with an immunological reaction to the test article.

- The target organs of toxicity were identified as the SC and IV injection sites. The injection site findings were not considered adverse and are clinically monitorable.
- Significant dose-dependent prolongations of aPTT were observed for males and females in all IV and SC treatment groups relative to control animals.
- Increased incidences and/or severity of minimal to slight SC hemorrhage and minimal to slight inflammatory cell infiltration at the SC injection sites (sites 1 and 2) were observed for males and females in the 200 mg/kg SC treatment groups relative to control animals.
- Minimal to slight lymphoid aggregates at the SC injection sites (sites 1 and 2) were observed for males and females in the 200 mg/kg SC treatment group.
- Increased incidences and/or severity of minimal to slight SC hemorrhage, minimal to slight inflammatory cell infiltration, and minimal dermal inflammatory cell infiltration at the venous injection site (left) was observed for females in the 100 mg/kg IV treatment group.
- Systemic exposure to garadacimab (C_{max} and AUC_{0-168}) generally increased in a dose proportional manner for males and females from 10 to 100 mg/kg IV and 60 to 200 mg/kg SC during the 26-week Treatment Period. Moderate accumulation of garadacimab was observed in the dosing groups during the study period (i.e., one-to-four-fold). No clear differences in systemic exposure between males and females were observed.
- NOAELs were identified as the mid doses (MDs) of 30 mg/kg/week (IV) and 60 mg/kg/week (SC) based upon deaths observed at 100 mg/kg/week (IV) and 200 mg/kg/week (SC).
